Q: What if the complaint is not sworn and signed? 
A: If a complaint is not sworn and signed, this is merely 
a formal defect that can be cured by having the 
complaint signed and sworn to by the person who filed 
it. 
 
Section 6. Sufficiency of complaint or information. — A 
complaint or information is sufficient if it states the 
name of the accused; the designation of the offense 
given by the statute; the acts or omissions 
complained of as constituting the offense; the name 

--- PAGE 21 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
21 
of the offended party; the approximate date of the 
commission of the offense; and the place where the 
offense was committed. 
 
When an offense is committed by more than one 
person, all of them shall be included in the complaint 
or information. (6a) 
 
Test for sufficiency of complaint or information 
The test is whether the crime is described in 
intelligible terms with such particularity as to 
apprise the accused with reasonable certainty of 
the offense charged to enable the accused to suitably 
prepare for his defense. 
 
The purpose of the enumeration of the matters which 
should be stated in the complaint or information for it 
to be sufficient, is to inform the accused of the nature 
and the cause of the accusation against him, a right 
guaranteed by no less than our Constitution. 
 
No matter how conclusive and convincing the 
evidence of guilt may be, an accused cannot be 
convicted of any offense unless it is charged in the 
information on which he is tried or is necessarily 
included therein.  
 
It must contain the following: 
1) name of the accused;  
2) designation of the offense given by the 
statute;  
3) acts or omissions complained of as 
constituting the offense;  
 most important  
4) name of the offended party;  
5) approximate date of the commission of the 
offense; and  
6) place where the offense was committed. 
7) when an offense is committed by more than 
one person, all of them shall be included in 
the complaint or information. 
 
Effect when the complaint or information is 
insufficient 
Where the complaint or information is insufficient, it 
cannot be the basis of any valid conviction 
 
Q: When should the question on the sufficiency of 
information be raised? 
A: Defect on the sufficiency of complaint or 
information should be raised during the course of trial, 
particularly during arraignment  
 
Q: What is the effect of failure to object on the defect 
of the complaint or information as regards the identity 
of the accused? 
A: Their failure to object to the alleged defect before 
entering their pleas of not guilty amounted to a waiver 
of the defect in the information. 
 
Q: Can an objection as to form or substance be raised 
for the first time on appeal? 
A: No. Objections as to matters of form or substance 
in the information cannot be made for the first time on 
appeal, and failure to object constitute a waiver 
 
Q: Can the appellate court dismiss the information for 
failure to raise the issue of defects in the information? 
A: Yes. The Court may motu propio dismiss the said 
information at the appellate stage, because he was not 
informed of the nature and cause of the accusation 
against him and the basic principles of due process.  
 
xx while a sufficiency of an information may be 
assailed, the right to question the sufficiency of the 
same is not absolute. Further, an accused is deemed 
to have waived the right to question the sufficiency 
of the complaint or information if he fails to object 
upon his arraignment or during trial. (Friyas v. People) 
 
The real nature of the criminal charge is determined 
not from the caption or the preamble of the 
information or from the specification of the 
provision of law alleged to have been violated which 
are mere conclusions of law, but by the actual 
recital of the facts in the complaint or 
information. 
 
Thus, even if the designation of the crime in the 
information is defective, what is controlling is the 
allegation of the facts in the information that 
comprises a crime and adequately describes the 
nature and the cause of the accusation against the 
accused. (Friyas v. People) 
 
Section 7. Name of the accused. — The complaint or 
information must state the name and surname of the 
accused or any appellation or nickname by which he 
has been or is known. If his name cannot be 
ascertained, he must be described under a fictitious 
name with a statement that his true name is 
unknown. 
 
If the true name of the accused is thereafter 
disclosed by him or appears in some other manner 
to the court, such true name shall be inserted in the 
complaint or information and record. (7a) 
 
Name of the accused 
1) Known - The complaint or information must 
state the name and surname of the accused or 
any appellation or nickname by which he has 
been or is known 
2) Not known - he must be described under a 
fictitious name with a statement that his true 
name is unknown. 
3) Unknown at first but thereafter disclosed or 
appeared - such true name shall be inserted in 
the complaint or information and record 
 
Q: What is the effect of erroneous designation of the 
name of the accused in the information? 
A: The erroneous designation of the name of the 
accused in the information will not vitiate the 

--- PAGE 22 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
22 
information filed, in so long as, the identity of the 
accused is clearly proven. 
 
Thus, in every criminal case, the task of the prosecution 
is always two-fold:  
1) To prove beyond reasonable doubt the 
commission of the crime charged.  
2) To establish with the same quantum of proof 
(proof beyond reasonable doubt) the identity 
of the person/s responsible therefore. 
 
Q: What is the consequence if another’s name is placed 
in the information? 
A: Information is not defective where another name, 
not the victim’s name, was placed in the information 
since the error is considered merely clerical and not 
substantial.  
 
Q: What is the effect if the accused enters his plea 
under a wrong name? 
A: An accused who entered a plea of not guilty under 
a wrong name is estopped from later on questioning 
court’s jurisdiction over his person 
 
The reluctance of the offended party in a rape case, 
in identifying the accused saying, "that it might be 
him,” which means that the private offended party 
is uncertain as to the identity of the accused, leads 
the Supreme Court to conclude that the identity of 
the accused was not proven. Even if the crime of 
rape was proven beyond reasonable doubt, the 
accused cannot be convicted based on an uncertain 
identification of his identity. However, this does 
not always mean that it is always supposed to be 
a direct evidence of the identity of the offender, 
because positive identification may be in two ways: 
1) A witness may identify a suspect or accused 
in a criminal case as the perpetrator of the 
crime as an eyewitness to the very act of 
the commission of the crime. This will 
constitute direct evidence. (DIRECT 
EVIDENCE) 
2) However, there may be instances where 
there may be a witness who did not actually 
see the very act or the commission of the 
crime but is still able to positively 
identify a suspect or accused as the 
perpetrator of the crime under the second 
type of identification, which forms part of 
circumstantial evidence, which, when taken 
together with all other pieces of evidence 
constituting an unbroken chain, leads to 
only one fair and reasonable conclusion: 
that the accused is the author of the 
crime, to the exclusion of all others. 
(CIRCUMSTANTIAL EVIDENCE) 
(People v. Tumambing) 
 
Section 11. Date of commission of the offense. — It is not 
necessary to state in the complaint or information 
the precise date the offense was committed except 
when it is a material ingredient of the offense. The 
offense may be alleged to have been committed on 
a date as near as possible to the actual date of its 
commission. (11a) 
 
GR: It is not necessary to state in the complaint or 
information the precise date of the commission of the 
offense since the offence may be alleged to have 
been committed on a date as near as possible to 
the actual date of its commission 
XPN: When it is necessary to state the precise date of 
the offense considering that the date and even 
sometimes the time is a material ingredient of the 
offense. 
 
Q: What is the effect of variance between the time 
alleged in the information and that established by 
evidence on trial? 
A: A variance between the time set out in the 
indictment and that establishment by the evidence 
during the trial does not constitute an error so serious 
as to warrant reversal of a conviction solely on that 
score. The only instance where the variance in the date 
of commission of the offense as alleged in the 
information and as established in evidence becomes 
fatal when the discrepancy is so great that it induces the 
perception that the information and the evidence are 
no longer pertaining to one and the same offense. 
 
Q: When should the date of the commission of the 
offense be alleged in the information? 
A: Only when such date is a material ingredient of the 
offense  
 
Q: What is the effect of failure to particularly allege in 
the information and prove the particular date of the 
commission of the crime? 
A: The failure of the prosecution to allege with 
particularity the date of the commission of the offense 
and worse, its failure to prove during the trial the date 
of the commission of the offense as alleged in the 
information, deprived the accused-appellant of the 
right to intelligently prepare for his defense and 
convincingly refute the charges against him 
 
Section 12. Name of the offended party. — The 
complaint or information must state the name and 
surname of the person against whom or against 
whose property the offense was committed, or any 
appellation or nickname by which such person has 
been or is known. If there is no better way of 
identifying him, he must be described under a 
fictitious name. 
 
(a) In offenses against property, if the name of the 
offended party is unknown, the property must be 
described with such particularity as to properly 
identify the offense charged. 
 
(b) If the true name of the of the person against 
whom or against whose properly the offense was 
committed is thereafter disclosed or ascertained, the 
court must cause the true name to be inserted in the 
complaint or information and the record.

sufficient to state its name, or any name or 
designation by which it is known or by which it may 
be identified, without need of averring that it is a 
juridical person or that it is organized in accordance 
with law. (12a) 
 
Name of the offended party

2) Not known - he must be described under a

XPN: robbery with violence and intimidation of 
persons – there must be allegations in the information 
of the name of the owner 
 
Q: What is the remedy of a person charged under an 
erroneous name or identity? 
A: The remedy of a person charged with a wrong name 
or identity is to file a motion to quash on the ground 
of lack of jurisdiction over his person. If he voluntarily 
appears at the arraignment without questioning the 
jurisdiction of the court over his person and pleads not 
guilty at the charge although under the erroneous 
name, the court acquires jurisdiction over him 
 
Section 8. Designation of the offense. — The complaint 
or information shall state the designation of the 
offense given by the statute, aver the acts or 
omissions constituting the offense, and specify its 
qualifying and aggravating circumstances. If there is 
no designation of the offense, reference shall be 
made to the section or subsection of the statute 
punishing it. (8a) 
 
Requirements of designation of offense 
1) state the designation of the offense given 
by the statute - character of the crime is 
determined neither by the caption or preamble 
of the information nor by the specification of 
the provision of law alleged to have been 
violated, they being conclusions of law, but by 
the actual recital of the ultimate facts and 
circumstances in the information (Licayco v. 
People) 
2) aver the acts or omissions constituting the 
offense – fatal; the allegations of facts 
constituting the offense charged are substantial 
matters and the right of an accused to question 
his conviction based on facts not alleged in the 
information cannot be waived 
3) specify its qualifying and aggravating 
circumstances – fatal; failure to state a 
qualifying or aggravating circumstance, even if 
duly proven at trial, will not be appreciated as 
such. (People v. Feliciano Jr) 
 
DESIGNATION OF OFFENSE 
The failure to designate the offense given by the 
statute or to mention the specific provision 
penalizing the act or an erroneous specification of 
the law violated does not vitiate the information if 
the facts alleged clearly recite the facts constituting 
the crime charged. What controls is not the title of 
the information or the designation of the 
offense, but the actual facts recited in the 
information. In other words, it is the recital of facts 
of the commission of the offense, not the 
nomenclature, that determines the crime being 
charged in the information. (Malto v. People) 
 
Another important rule in the allegation of or the 
recital of the acts or omissions in the body of the 
information – the specific acts of the accused do not 
have to be described in detail in the information as 
it is enough that the offense be described with 
sufficient particularity to make sure the accused 
fully understands what he is being charged with. 
The particularity must be such that a person of 
ordinary intelligence immediately knows what the 
charge is. Moreover, reasonable certainty of the 
crime suffices (Guy v. People). 
 
AVERMENT OF ACTS AND OMMISSIONS 
Q: what if the acts or omissions and the circumstances 
recited in the information are vague or ambiguous or it 
leads to a conclusion of two or more crimes being 
charged? 
A:  
1) If there is a designation of the offense or there 
is a section or subsection specified on what law 
is violated - the designation of the offense or 
the specification of the provision of law that is 
violated will control. 
2) If there is no designation of the offense or 
there is no specification of a section or 
subsection of a law that is violated and there is 
ambiguity in the recital of the acts or omissions 
in the body of the information, the remedies of 
the accused would be: 
 Ask a bill of particulars 
 File a motion to quash the information 
for failure to state or charge an offense

24 
 
Q: What is the main purpose of alleging in the 
information the elements constituting the offense? 
A: The main purpose of requiring the various elements 
of a crime to be set out in the information is to enable 
the accused to suitably prepare his defense because he 
is presumed to have no independent knowledge of 
the facts that constitute the offense 
 
SPECIFICATION OF QUALIFYING AND 
AGRRAVATING CIRCUMSTANCES 
1) Qualifying - changes the nature of the crime 
and thus increases the degree of the penalty 
2) Aggravating - changes the nature of the crime 
and thus increases the period of the imposable 
penalty 
 
A qualifying circumstance need not be preceded by 
descriptive words such as “qualifying” or “qualified 
by” to properly qualify an offense. It is not the use 
of the words “qualifying” or “qualified by” that 
raises a crime to a higher category, but the specific 
allegation of an attendant circumstance which adds 
the essential element raising the crime to a higher 
category. As regards aggravating circumstances, the 
mere statement of for example “with intent to kill 
qualified by treachery or evident premeditation and 
abuse of superior strength”, any which one of the 
aggravating circumstances in the RPC is not enough 
if the complaint or information fails to adequately 
describe the circumstances of the aggravating 
circumstances. It must be, in some way, described 
with sufficient particularity although the threshold 
of the specificity is just that an ordinary person of 
ordinary intelligence may immediately know what 
that particular allegation purports to be. (People v. Rosas) 
 
It is sufficient to allege conspiracy as a mode of 
commission of an offense in either of the following 
manners:  
1) By the use of the word “conspire” or is 
derivatives or synonyms such as 
“confederate”, “connive”, “collude”, etc.  
2) By allegation of basic facts constituting the 
conspiracy in a manner that a person of 
common understanding would know what 
was intended and with such precision that 
would enable the accused to competently 
enter a plea to a subsequent indictment of 
the same facts In the case cited, the words 
used to allege conspiracy is the phrase 
“participate in and facilitate” so 
according to the SC, the phrase “participate 
in and facilitate” is a clear and definite 
allegation of conspiracy sufficient for the 
accused to competently make a plea and 
make a proper defense. (Francisco v. 
People) 
 
The accused cannot be convicted of qualified rape 
even if it is proven during trial because the qualifying 
circumstance was not alleged in the information. 
(People v. Mendoza) 
 
During the course of the trial, the prosecution was 
able to prove that the dwelling of the subject of 
arson was inhabited. It could have qualified the 
arson, but it was not stated in the information that 
the dwelling was inhabited. Then the court cannot 
impose the penalty higher than that of arson since it 
cannot be qualified for failure to state the qualifying 
circumstance in the information. (Buebos v. People) 
 
The allegation that the offender is the victim’s uncle 
is not sufficient since the wordings of the statute 
provides that there must be an allegation that if it’s 
not within the specific list, there must be an 
allegation that the relative is the relative by 
consanguinity or affinity within the 3rd civil degree. 
(People v. Ubiña) 
 
The Supreme court said that it is not enough that in 
the recital of the information there are 3 accused. 
There should be a specification that the crime was 
committed by 2 or more persons. Aside from the 
general averment that there are 3 accused, there 
must be a special mention that it is qualified or is 
aggravated because it is committed by 2 or more 
persons. (People v. Alarcon) 
 
Supreme Court held further that even if you cannot 
premise the award of exemplary damages to the 
presence of aggravating circumstance, if the crime 
committed is highly reprehensible or outrageous 
conduct, then exemplary damages can also be 
awarded. (People v. Dalisay) 
 
The allegation in the Information that Maries was his 
minor daughter is as a matter of law insufficient to 
alert him as to the exact nature of the rape imputed 
to him. To do so would be to allege a conclusion of 
law to which the plea of not guilty joins no issue. Its 
effect is the same as alleging negligence without 
specifying the facts constituting such want of care or 
prudence. In the instant case, the exact age of the 
victim should have been asserted. Furthermore, 
the term minority is too technical to satisfy the 
pleading requirement that acts alleged as crime 
must be averred in a manner that a person of 
common understanding would understand the 
offense being charged. Certainly stating the words 
fifteen (15) years old, for example, in the 
information would signify something more familiar 
than the word minority could achieve. (People v. 
Mascarinas) 
 
REFERENCE AS TO SECTION OR 
SUBSECTION OF THE STATUTE 
Q: When can reference to the section or subsection of 
statute be alleged in the information? 
A: It is only when there is no specific name given to 
the offense that reference to the section or subsection 
of the statute punishing it may be made, and this 
actually applies to offenses under special laws

Section 9. Cause of the accusation. — The acts or 
omissions complained of as constituting the offense 
and the qualifying and aggravating circumstances 
must be stated in ordinary and concise language and 
not necessarily in the language used in the statute but 
in terms sufficient to enable a person of common 
understanding to know what offense is being 
charged as well as its qualifying and aggravating 
circumstances and for the court to pronounce 
judgment. (9a) 
 
How to allege in the complaint or information 
1) must be stated in ordinary and concise 
language 
2) not necessarily in the language used in the 
statute but in terms sufficient to enable a 
person of common understanding to know 
what offense is being charged as well as its 
qualifying and aggravating circumstances and 
for the court to pronounce judgment 
 
This provision prescribes a manner by which acts or 
omissions as well as aggravating and qualifying 
circumstances should be alleged in the complaint or 
information. The main purpose of the requirement that 
the acts or omissions complained of as constituting an 
offense must be stated in ordinary and concise 
language is to enable a person of common 
understanding to know what the offense is intended to 
be charged so that he could suitably prepare for his 
defense. 
 
Section 9 of Rule 110 actually complements Rule 110 
Section 8 on the designation of the offense because in 
section 9 it specifies how you have to allege or to recite 
the acts and omissions constituting the crime being 
charged or the qualifying or aggravating circumstance. 
 
In People v. Mauricio, the Court elucidated: 
 
"The use of the word 'must' indicates that the 
requirement is mandatory, therefore failure to 
comply with Sec. 9, Rule 110, means that generic 
aggravating circumstances, although proven at the 
trial, cannot be appreciated against the accused if 
such circumstances are not stated in the information. 
It is a cardinal rule that rules of criminal procedure 
are given retroactive application insofar as they 
benefit the accused." (People v. Suela) 
 
The information must allege clearly and accurately 
the elements of the crime charged. The facts and 
circumstances are necessary to be included therein 
must be determined by reference to the definition 
and elements of the specific crimes being charged. 
(Serapio v. Sandiganbayan) 
 
Since the information did not specifically allege the 
circumstance that the victim was under PNP custody 
during the rape, the trial court cannot appreciate the 
qualifying circumstance and thus can only impose 
the penalty for simple rape. (People v. Aure) 
 
That the accused is the mother of the victim was not 
alleged, therefore it cannot be taken into 
consideration as a qualifying circumstance. (People v. Tampus) 
 
OTHER RULES 
Libel cases 
GR: information must set out the particular 
defamatory words verbatim and as published and that 
the statement of their substance is insufficient. 
XPN: may be cured by evidence presented in court 
XPN of XPN: accused objects the presentation of 
evidence 
 
Offenses in relation to public office 
GR: if the offense charged against a public officer is 
not one of those specifically mentioned under the 
Sandiganbayan law but comes under the offenses in 
general which may be committed in relation to 
public office, then a sufficient description of how that 
particular offense was committed in relation to office 
must be recited in the complaint or information. 
XPN: if the offense cannot be committed unless it 
is in relation to office. There is no necessity to 
specifically allege that it was indeed committed in 
relation to office. It will be a mere superfluity. 
 
Allegations of negativing exceptions 
1) Where the statute alleged to have been violated 
applies only to a specific class of persons and 
to special conditions, the complaint or 
information must allege facts establishing the 
accused falls within the specific class affected 
and not those exempted from the coverage of 
the law. Otherwise, the complaint or 
information is quashable for failure to state 
facts constituting an offense 
2) Where the statute penalizes generally the 
acts therein defined and is intended to 
apply to all persons indiscriminately 
although some special situations are excepted 
from its coverage, the complaint or 
information is sufficient even if it does not 
allege that the accused falls within the 
excepted situation for then the complete 
definition of the offense is entirely separable 
from the exceptions and can be made without 
reference to the latter. If it falls within the 
excepted class, the accused may raise is such 
fact as a defense. 
 
Q: What is the effect if the aggravating circumstance is 
alleged in the information? 
A: Award of exemplary damages is justified if an 
aggravating circumstance, either qualifying or generic, 
accompanies the crime 
 
Furthermore, in People v. Catubig, we held that while 
a non-alleged but proven aggravating 
circumstance cannot be used to increase the 
penalty, nonetheless it can be the source of civil

  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
26 
awards. Hence, we retain the trial court's civil grants 
in this regard. (People v. Suela) 
 
Section 13. Duplicity of the offense. — A complaint or 
information must charge but one offense, except 
when the law prescribes a single punishment for 
various offenses. (13a) 
 
Duplicitous complaints or information 
Rule 
1) Complaint filed in court – duplicitous 
complaints apply 
2) Complaint filed to the prosecutor’s office - 
duplicitous complaints do not apply 
 
GR: The rule prohibits charging of two or more 
offenses in a single information has for its aim to give 
the defendant the necessary knowledge of the charge 
to enable him to prepare for his defense. The State shall 
not heap upon the defendant two or more charges 
which might confuse him in his defense 
XPN: when the law prescribes a single punishment for 
various offenses such as: 
1) Complex crime - a single act constitutes two or 
more grave or less grave felonies or when an 
offense is a necessary means in committing the 
other 
 Compound crime - a single act 
constitutes two or more grave or less 
grave felonies. 
 Complex crime proper - offense is a 
necessary means in the commission of 
the other 
 
Q: What is the requirement in case an accused is 
charged with a complex crime? 
A: The information charging a complex crime should 
allege each element of the component offenses with 
the same precision that would be necessary if they were 
made the subject of a separate prosecution 
 
2) Special complex crime - crimes specifically 
named or designated in special penal offenses 
or under the Revised Penal Code all of which 
are punished with one penalty 
 
Special complex 
crime 
Ordinary complex 
crimes 
specifically named or 
designated in special 
penal offenses or 
under the Revised 
Penal Code 
applies specifically to 
felonies defined 
under the Revised 
Penal Code and is 
governed by Article 
48 of the RPC 
 
3) Continuous crimes – (delito continuado), single 
larceny doctrine, a series of act arising from a 
single criminal intent. 
 
For delito continuado to exist there should be a 
plurality of acts performed during a period of 
time; unity of penal provision violated; and unity 
of criminal intent or purpose, which means that 
two or more violations of the same penal provisions 
are united in one and same instant or resolution 
leading to the perpetration of the same criminal 
purpose or aim (Santiago v. Garchitorena) 
 
Applying the concept of delito continuado, we 
treated as constituting only one offense the 
following cases: 
 
(1) The theft of 13 cows belonging to two different 
owners committed by the accused at the same time 
and at the same period of time (People v. Tumlos). 
(2) The theft of six roosters belonging to two 
different owners from the same coop and at the 
same period of time (People v. Jaranillo). 
(3) The theft of two roosters in the same place and 
on the same occasion (People v. De Leon). 
(4) The illegal charging of fees for services rendered 
by a lawyer every time he collects veteran's benefits 
on behalf of a client, who agreed that the attorney's 
fees shall be paid out of said benefits (People v. Sabbun). The collection of the legal fees were 
impelled by the same motive, that of collecting fees 
for services rendered, and all acts of collection were 
made under the same criminal impulse (People v. Lawas). 
 
On the other hand, we declined to apply the concept 
to the following cases: 
 
(1) Two estafa cases, one of which was committed 
during the period from January 19 to December 
1955 and the other from January 1956 to July 1956 
(People v. Dichupa). The said acts were committed 
on two different occasions. 
(2) Several malversations committed in May, June 
and July, 1936, and falsifications to conceal said 
offenses committed in August and October 1936. 
The malversations and falsifications "were not the 
result of only one purpose or of only one resolution 
to embezzle and falsify . . ." (People v. Cid). 
(3) Two estafa cases, one committed in December 
1963 involving the failure of the collector to turn 
over the installments for a radio and the other in 
June 1964 involving the pocketing of the 
installments for a sewing machine (People v. 
Ledesma). 
(4) 75 estafa cases committed by the conversion by 
the agent of collections from customers of the 
employer made on different dates (Gamboa v. 
Court of Appeals). (Santiago v. Garchitorena) 
 
The accused said that he cannot be charged with 
multiple rapes since the rapes occurred within just 
few minutes from each other. He argued that he was 
just motivated by a single criminal intent although 
there were breaks in between the several rapes he 
committed. The Supreme Court did not agree and 
held that when the accused would rest after carnal 
knowledge or after satisfying his lust upon the victim 
and within a sufficient time to regain his strength 
would do it again, to do the rape again the carnal 
knowledge to the victim, it can be inferred that the 
accused has decided to commit separate and distinct 

--- PAGE 27 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
27 
acts of rape and that he was not merely motivated by 
a single impulse but rather by several criminal 
intents. (People v. Lucena) 
 
XPN of XPN: if there will be double jeopardy 
 
Cases involving loose firearms 
REPUBLIC ACT NO. 10591 
AN ACT PROVIDING FOR A 
COMPREHENSIVE LAW ON FIREARMS 
AND AMMUNITION AND PROVIDING 
PENALTIES FOR VIOLATIONS THEREOF 
SEC. 29. Use of Loose Firearm in the Commission of a Crime. 
– The use of a loose firearm, when inherent in the 
commission of a crime punishable under the Revised 
Penal Code or other special laws, shall be considered 
as an aggravating circumstance: Provided, That if 
the crime committed with the use of a loose firearm is 
penalized by the law with a maximum penalty which 
is lower than that prescribed in the preceding section 
for illegal possession of firearm, the penalty for illegal 
possession of firearm shall be imposed in lieu of 
the penalty for the crime charged: Provided, further, 
That if the crime committed with the use of a loose 
firearm is penalized by the law with a maximum 
penalty which is equal to that imposed under the 
preceding section for illegal possession of firearms, the 
penalty of prision mayor in its minimum period shall be 
imposed in addition to the penalty for the crime 
punishable under the Revised Penal Code or other 
special laws of which he/she is found guilty. 
 
If the violation of this Act is in furtherance of, or 
incident to, or in connection with the crime of 
rebellion of insurrection, or attempted coup d’ 
etat, such violation shall be absorbed as an element of 
the crime of rebellion or insurrection, or attempted 
coup d’ etat. 
 
If the crime is committed by the person without 
using the loose firearm, the violation of this Act shall 
be considered as a distinct and separate offense. 
 
SUMMARY: 
Penalties 
GR: The maximum penalty shall be imposed when the 
use of a loose firearm is inherent in the commission 
of a crime punishable under the Revised Penal Code 
or other special laws. 
XPNs: (PENALTY SHOULD BE WHICHEVER 
IS HIGHER BETWEEN RPC/SPECIAL LAW 
PENALTY AND PENALTY UNDER ILLEGAL 
POSSESSION OF FIREARMS) 
1) maximum penalty is lower than that prescribed 
in the preceding section for illegal possession 
of firearm - penalty for illegal possession of 
firearm shall be imposed  
2) maximum penalty which is equal to that 
imposed under the preceding section for illegal 
possession of firearms - penalty of prision 
mayor in its minimum period shall be 
imposed in addition to the penalty for the 
crime punishable under the Revised Penal 
Code or other special laws of which he/she 
is found guilty 
 
Other circumstances 
1) If the violation of this Act is in furtherance 
of, or incident to, or in connection with the 
crime of rebellion of insurrection, or 
attempted coup d’ etat - such violation shall 
be absorbed as an element of the crime of 
rebellion or insurrection, or attempted coup d’ 
etat. 
2) If the crime is committed by the person 
without using the loose firearm - the violation 
of this Act shall be considered as a distinct 
and separate offense. 
 
The Supreme Court said each illegal possession of a 
different type of firearm shall be prosecuted 
separately and not together since the law punishes 
the different type of firearm with a different penalty. 
It will not suffice to lump all the seized firearms in 
one information because the different penalties are 
imposed by law depending on the caliber of the 
weapon seized. To lump them altogether is 
duplicitous. (Fajardo v. People) 
 
Q: What is the effect of the failure to prove a complex 
crime?  
A: It is well settled that where a complex crime is 
charged and evidence fails to support the charge as to 
one of the component offenses, the defendant can be 
convicted only of the offense proved. 
 
Q: What is the remedy against a duplicitous 
information? A: Non-compliance with the Rule is a 
ground for the quashing of a duplicitous complaint or 
information under Rule 117 of the Revised Rules on 
Criminal Procedure. 
 
Waiver 
The failure to object to a duplicitous information 
before he enters his plea results in a waiver of the right 
to object to it and the court may thereafter convict him 
of as many offenses as are charged and proved, and 
impose on him the penalty for each offense setting out 
separately the findings of fact and law in each offense. 
 
Section 15. Place where action is to be instituted. — 
 
(a) Subject to existing laws, the criminal action shall 
be instituted and tried in the court of the 
municipality or territory where the offense was 
committed or where any of its essential ingredients 
occurred. 
(b) Where an offense is committed in a train, aircraft, 
or other public or private vehicle while in the course 
of its trip, the criminal action shall be instituted and 
tried in the court of any municipality or territory 
where such train, aircraft or other vehicle passed 
during such its trip, including the place of its 
departure and arrival. 

--- PAGE 28 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
28 
(c) Where an offense is committed on board a vessel 
in the course of its voyage, the criminal action shall 
be instituted and tried in the court of the first port 
of entry or of any municipality or territory where the 
vessel passed during such voyage, subject to the 
generally accepted principles of international law. 
(d) Crimes committed outside the Philippines but 
punishable under Article 2 of the Revised Penal 
Code shall be cognizable by the court where the 
criminal action is first filed. (15a) 
 
Venue of criminal actions 
Venue does not only determine the place where the 
criminal action is to be constituted, but also the court 
which has jurisdiction to try and hear the case. When a 
court is found to have no jurisdiction over the territory, 
the case can be dismissed at any time. 
 
Two-Fold Reason for the Rule:  
1) The jurisdiction of the trial court is limited to 
well-defined territories, such that a trial court 
can only hear and try cases involving crimes 
committed within their territorial jurisdiction.  
2) Laying the venue in the locus criminis is 
grounded on the necessity and justice of having 
an accused on trial in the municipality or 
province where witnesses and other facilities 
for his defense are available

Power of the SC to change venue or place 
Section 5 (4) Article VIII, 1987 Constitution 
SECTION 5. The Supreme Court shall have the 
following powers:  
xxx 
4) Order a change of venue or place of trial to avoid 
a miscarriage of justice. 
 
In applying the standards of miscarriage of justice, the 
Court will evaluate which court would be in a better 
position to serve the interest of justice, taking into 
account  
1) The nature of the controversy;  
2) Accessibility of the court to the parties; and  
3) Other similar factor

Section 14. Amendment or substitution. — A complaint 
or information may be amended, in form or in 
substance, without leave of court, at any time before 
the accused enters his plea. After the plea and during 
the trial, a formal amendment may only be made 

--- PAGE 30 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
30 
with leave of court and when it can be done without 
causing prejudice to the rights of the accused. 
 
However, any amendment before plea, which 
downgrades the nature of the offense charged in or 
excludes any accused from the complaint or 
information, can be made only upon motion by the 
prosecutor, with notice to the offended party and 
with leave of court. The court shall state its reasons 
in resolving the motion and copies of its order shall 
be furnished all parties, especially the offended party. 
(n) 
 
If it appears at any time before judgment that a 
mistake has been made in charging the proper 
offense, the court shall dismiss the original 
complaint or information upon the filing of a new 
one charging the proper offense in accordance with 
section 19, Rule 119, provided the accused shall not 
be placed in double jeopardy. The court may require 
the witnesses to give bail for their appearance at the 
trial. (14a)

Q: What is the TEST to determine whether or not an 
accused will be prejudiced by an amendment? 
A: The test as to when the rights of an accused are 
prejudiced with the amendment of the complaint or 
information is when the defense under the 
complaint or information as originally stood will 
no longer be available after the amendment is 
made, and when any evidence the accused might 
have would be inapplicable to the complaint or 
information as amended. 
 
Amendment – refers to the modification of a 
complaint or information by the public prosecutor 
which changes its form or substance. It can be: 
1) Formal – does not change the nature of the 
offense 
2) Substantial – changes the nature of the offense 
 
Formal amendments  
Gabionsa v CA 
1) It does not deprive the accused of the right to 
invoke prescription; 
2) It does not affect or alter the nature of the 
offense originally charged 
3) It does not involve a change in the basic theory 
of the prosecution so as to require the accused 
to undergo any material change or 
modification in his defense. 
4) It does not expose the accused to a charge 
which would call for a higher penalty; and 
5) It does not cause surprise nor deprive the 
accused of an opportunity to meet the new 
averment 
Ricarze v. CA 
6) New allegations which relate only to the range 
of penalty that the court may impose in the 
event of a conviction; 
7) An amendment which does not charge another 
offense different or distinct from that charged 
in the original one; 
8) Additional allegation which does not alter the 
prosecution's theory of the case so as to cause 
surprise to the accused and affect the form of 
defense he has or will assume; 
9) An amendment which does not adversely 
affect any substantial right of the accused; and 
10) An amendment that merely adds specifications 
to eliminate vagueness in the information and 
not to introduce new and material facts and 
merely state with additional precision 
something which is already contained in the 
original information, and which adds nothing 
essential for conviction for the crime charged. 
Pacoy v. Judge Cajigal 
11) Amendment to change the designation of the 
offense from homicide to murder - based on 
the facts and the circumstances of this case, 
what was originally charged was actually 
murder. 
Almeda v. Judge Villaluz 
12) Adding of the allegation of habitual 
delinquency and recidivism in the original 
information involving qualified theft - only 
changes the penalty which later on may be 
imposed on the accused 
Tehankee v. Madayag 
13) From frustrated murder to murder 
Buhat v. CA 
14) additional allegation of conspiracy where the 
participation of the accused as a principal 
 
Substantial amendments 
1) An amendment which changes the manner of 
the commission of the offense  
2) An amendment which changes the name of the 
offended party in cases where the identity of 
the offended party is an element or is a 
necessary ingredient of the crime charged 
3) An amendment to change the date of the 
commission of the offense over a wide span 

--- PAGE 31 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
31 
of time (more than 3 years). So long as it is 
within the range of the change in the date, then 
it will just be considered as a formal 
amendment 
4) An amendment for the purpose of making the 
information charge an offense when the 
original information does not charge any 
offense. 
5) An amendment which changes the fact or 
ground of responsibility of the accused. 
Montenegro v. People 
6) From robbery to robbery in an uninhabited 
place 
People v. Sandoval 
7) Amendment in the relationship of the accused 
to the victim in rape cases 
 
Objection to the information or complaint must be 
placed at the time the amendment is made, 
otherwise, accused silence would be deemed consent 
on his part to the amendment. (People v. Janairo) 
 
A careful scrutiny of the rule on amendment, 
although it uses singular word complaint or 
information it doesn’t mean that two or more 
complaints or information cannot be amended into 
only one information. Surely, such could not have 
been intended by the SC. Otherwise, there can be an 
absurd situation where two or more complaints or 
information could no longer be amended into one 
information (Fronda-Baggao v. People) 
 
SUBSTITUTION 
If it appears at any time before judgment that a 
mistake has been made in charging the proper 
offense, the court shall dismiss the original 
complaint or information upon the filing of a 
new one charging the proper offense in 
accordance with section 19, Rule 119, provided the 
accused shall not be placed in double jeopardy. The 
court may require the witnesses to give bail for their 
appearance at the trial. (14a) 
 
RULE 119 
Sec. 19. When mistake has been made in charging the proper 
offense. – When it becomes manifest at any time 
before judgment that a mistake has been made in 
charging the proper offense and the accused 
cannot be convicted of the offense charged or 
any other offense necessarily included therein, 
the accused shall not be discharged if there 
appears good cause to detain him. In such case, 
the court shall commit the accused to answer for the 
proper offense and dismiss the original case upon 
the filing of the proper information.  
 
There can only be a substitution of the offense charged 
is wholly different from the offense that is proven. If 
not wholly different, there may be double jeopardy.  
 
When substitution is proper, the court will dismiss the 
original charge or the original information only upon 
the filing of the new information charging the proper 
offense. 
 
Test in determining the identity of the offenses  
There is an identity between the two offenses  
1) when the evidences to support a conviction 
of one offense would be sufficient to 
warrant a conviction to the other or  
2) when the second offense is exactly the 
same as the first or  
3) when the second offense is an attempt to 
commit or frustration of or when it 
necessarily includes or necessarily 
included in the offense charged in the 
first information

new information 
involves a different

hence, the accused 
cannot claim double 
jeopardy 
 
RULE 111 
PROSECUTION OF CIVIL ACTION 
 
Section 1. Institution of criminal and civil actions. — (a) 
When a criminal action is instituted, the civil action 
for the recovery of civil liability arising from the 
offense charged shall be deemed instituted with the 
criminal action unless the offended party waives the 

--- PAGE 32 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
32 
civil action, reserves the right to institute it separately 
or institutes the civil action prior to the criminal 
action. 
 
The reservation of the right to institute separately 
the civil action shall be made before the prosecution 
starts presenting its evidence and under 
circumstances affording the offended party a 
reasonable opportunity to make such reservation. 
 
When the offended party seeks to enforce civil 
liability against the accused by way of moral, 
nominal, temperate, or exemplary damages without 
specifying the amount thereof in the complaint or 
information, the filing fees thereof shall constitute a 
first lien on the judgment awarding such damages. 
 
Where the amount of damages, other than actual, is 
specified in the complaint or information, the 
corresponding filing fees shall be paid by the 
offended party upon the filing thereof in court. 
 
Except as otherwise provided in these Rules, no 
filing fees shall be required for actual damages. 
 
No counterclaim, cross-claim or third-party 
complaint may be filed by the accused in the criminal 
case, but any cause of action which could have been 
the subject thereof may be litigated in a separate civil 
action. (1a) 
 
(b) The criminal action for violation of Batas 
Pambansa Blg. 22 shall be deemed to include the 
corresponding civil action. No reservation to file 
such civil action separately shall be allowed. 
 
Upon filing of the aforesaid joint criminal and civil 
actions, the offended party shall pay in full the filing 
fees based on the amount of the check involved, 
which shall be considered as the actual damages 
claimed. Where the complaint or information also 
seeks to recover liquidated, moral, nominal, 
temperate or exemplary damages, the offended party 
shall pay additional filing fees based on the amounts 
alleged therein. If the amounts are not so alleged but 
any of these damages are subsequently awarded by 
the court, the filing fees based on the amount 
awarded shall constitute a first lien on the judgment. 
 
Where the civil action has been filed separately and 
trial thereof has not yet commenced, it may be 
consolidated with the criminal action upon 
application with the court trying the latter case. If the 
application is granted, the trial of both actions shall 
proceed in accordance with section 2 of this Rule 
governing consolidation of the civil and criminal 
actions. (cir. 57-97) 
 
Two aspects of a criminal action 
1) Criminal 
2) Civil – civil liability ex delicto 
 
Civil liability ex delicto 
Art. 104. What is included in civil liability. — The civil 
liability established in Articles 100, 101, 102, and 103 
of this Code includes: 
1) Restitution; 
2) Reparation of the damage caused; 
3) Indemnification for consequential damages. 
 
The substantive basis for this two-pronged aspect of a 
criminal action is Article 100 of the Revised Penal 
Code. 
 
Art. 100. Civil liability of a person guilty of felony. — 
Every person criminally liable for a felony is also 
civilly liable. 
 
GR: When a criminal action is instituted, the civil 
action for the recovery of civil liability arising from the 
offense charged shall be deemed instituted with the 
criminal action 
 
XPN:  
1) When the offended party waives the civil action 
2) When he reserves the right to institute the civil 
action separately –  
Purpose: The purpose of this rule requiring 
reservation is to prevent the offended party 
from recovering damages twice for the same 
act or omission 
Effect: loses his right to intervene in the 
prosecution of the criminal case for this reason 
he no longer has any right nor should he be 
expected to move for reconsideration of much 
less appeal from the decision in the criminal 
case insofar as it decides the question of civil 
indemnity 
3) When he institutes the civil action prior to the 
criminal action  
Effect: The effect of the institution of the civil 
action prior to the criminal action is that the 
private offended party has abandoned his right 
to press recovery for the civil liability ex delicto 
in the criminal case and has opted instead to 
recover them in a separate civil action. 
4) When the civil action is based on Article 32, 33, 
34 and 2176 of the Civil Code otherwise 
known as independent civil action 
Effect: the offended party can file two separate 
suit for the same act or omission.  
1) The first is a criminal case where the civil 
action to recover civil liability ex delicto is 
deemed instituted.  
2) And the other is a civil case for the quasi-
delict without violating the rule on non-
forum shopping. 
 
No reservation allowed 
1) The civil action arising from violation of Batas 
Pambansa Blg. 22. 
XPN: The following are allowed 
 To waive civil action ex delicto in 
relation to the BP 22 case 2.  

--- PAGE 33 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
33 
 To file the civil action in relation to BP 
22 case prior to the institution of the 
criminal action for BP 22. 
 
When you file the BP 22 case, since the civil liability 
ex delicto is deemed instituted, the civil case that you 
filed prior will be dismissible. It has to be litigated in 
the criminal action because of the rules that provides 
that the civil liability ex delicto is deemed instituted 
in the BP 22 case. 
 
The proper action would be to consolidate the 
previously filed civil action in relation to the action 
in the B.P. 22 case and consolidate it to the criminal 
action that was subsequently filed. (Hyatt 
Industrial v. Asia Dynamic) 
 
There can be no separate, independent civil action 
grounded on fraud but the allegation is the violation 
of B.P. 22 because of the rule that the civil liability 
ex delicto is deemed instituted in the criminal action, 
there is no reservation allowed. Even an 
independent civil action even if phrased as fraud, as 
provided under Article 33 of the Civil Code, the case 
will not be allowed because essentially it would still 
be recovery of the amount subject to B.P. 22 case. 
(Heirs of Simon v. Chan) 
 
2) The civil action for the recovery of civil liability 
arising from cases falling under the jurisdiction 
of the Sandiganbayan or other appropriate 
courts involving public officers. 
3) The civil action for the recovery of the civil 
liability for taxes and penalties in criminal 
offenses arising from violations of the National 
Internal Revenue Code (NIRC) or Tariff and 
Customs Code and other laws administered by 
the Bureau of Internal Revenue or Bureau of 
Customs 
 
Filing fees and other damages 
Filing fees 
GR: Actual damages – no filing fees 
XPN: BP 22 - Upon filing of the aforesaid joint 
criminal and civil actions, the offended party shall pay 
in full the filing fees based on the amount of the 
check involved, which shall be considered as the 
actual damages claimed. 
 
Indemnification for consequential damages 
1) Moral 
2) Exemplary 
3) Nominal 
4) Temperate 
5) Actual 
 
GR: To all other cases, when the offended party seeks 
to enforce civil liability against the accused by way of 
moral, nominal, temperate, or exemplary damages 
without specifying the amount thereof in the complaint 
or information, the filing fees thereof shall constitute 
a first lien on the judgment awarding such 
damages. 
XPN: the damages is specified in amount and the 
other damages were eventually given to the court but 
there was no specification in the complaint or 
information - the corresponding filing fee shall be paid 
by the offended party upon the filing thereof in court. 
 
Rule in BP 22 cases 
1) The actual damages is the amount that it 
reflected in the check subject of the BP 22 case 
- there is corresponding filing fees in BP 22 
cases. 
2) if nominal damages, moral damages, temperate 
damages or exemplary damages are specifically 
alleged with specific amount - pay the 
corresponding filing fees at the start or at the 
institution of the criminal action 
3) if nominal damages, moral damages, temperate 
damages or exemplary damages are specifically 
alleged without specific amount - after the 
award is granted, the filing fees, based on the 
amount awarded shall constitute as a first lien 
on the judgment 
 
Damages when death occurs 
In relation to the award of damages when death occurs 
as a result of the crime, the heirs of the deceased are 
entitled to the following items of damages:  
1) Indemnity for the death of the victim of the 
offense;  
2) Indemnity for the loss of earning capacity of 
the deceased;  
3) Moral damages for mental anguish;  
4) Exemplary damages; and  
5) Attorney’s fees and expenses of litigation. 
6) Interest may also be granted by the court in 
proper cases. 
 
Counterclaim, cross-claim or third-party 
complaint 
No counterclaim, cross-claim or third-party 
complaint may be filed by the accused in the criminal 
case, but any cause of action which could have been 
the subject thereof may be litigated in a separate civil 
action. 
 
Counterclaim - any claim which a defending party 
may have against an opposing party 
Cross-claim – any claim of a certain amount or the 
whole amount or a course of action against co-
defendant 
Third-party complaint - a claim that it, if any, party 
may with leave of court file against a person, not a party 
to the action called a third party defendant for 
contribution, indemnity, subrogation or any other 
relief in respect of his or her opponents’ claim. 
 
Counterclaim, cross claim, third party complaint the 
course of action of the offender or the accused can

proceed simultaneously and independently of the 
criminal action. 
 
Consolidation of the civil and criminal actions 
A situation wherein the civil action is filed separately in 
the trial of the civil action has not yet commenced. 
It may then be consolidated with the criminal action 
upon application with a court trying the latter case. 
 
Section 2. When separate civil action is suspended. — 
After the criminal action has been commenced, the 
separate civil action arising therefrom cannot be 
instituted until final judgment has been entered in 
the criminal action. 
 
If the criminal action is filed after the said civil action 
has already been instituted, the latter shall be 
suspended in whatever stage it may be found before 
judgment on the merits. The suspension shall last 
until final judgment is rendered in the criminal 
action. Nevertheless, before judgment on the merits 
is rendered in the civil action, the same may, upon 
motion of the offended party, be consolidated with 
the criminal action in the court trying the criminal 
action. In case of consolidation, the evidence already 
adduced in the civil action shall be deemed 
automatically reproduced in the criminal action 
without prejudice to the right of the prosecution to 
cross-examine the witnesses presented by the 
offended party in the criminal case and of the parties 
to present additional evidence. The consolidated 
criminal and civil actions shall be tried and decided 
jointly. 
 
During the pendency of the criminal action, the 
running of the period of prescription of the civil 
action which cannot be instituted separately or 
whose proceeding has been suspended shall be 
tolled. (n) 
 
The extinction of the penal action does not carry 
with it extinction of the civil action. However, the 
civil action based on delict shall be deemed 
extinguished if there is a finding in a final judgment 
in the criminal action that the act or omission from 
which the civil liability may arise did not exist. (2a) 
 
Commencement of criminal or civil action 
1) Criminal      Separate civil action - cannot be 
instituted until final judgment has been entered 
in the criminal action 
2) Separate civil action          Criminal - the latter 
shall be suspended in whatever state it may be 
found before judgment on the merits. The 
suspension shall last until final judgment is 
rendered in the criminal action. 
 
Reliefs while the civil action is suspended 
1) consolidate the civil action in the criminal 
action Effects:  
 the evidence already adduced in the 
civil action shall be deemed 
automatically reproduced in the 
criminal action  
 right of the prosecution to cross-
examine the witnesses presented by the 
offended party in the criminal case  
 right to present additional evidence. 
2) power to issue preliminary and auxiliary writs 
such as preliminary injunction, attachment, 
appointment of a receiver, fixing the amount 
of bonds to be filed, and other processes of 
similar nature none of which goes into the 
merits of the case 
3) During the pendency of the criminal action, the 
running period of prescription of the civil 
action which cannot be instituted separately or 
whose proceeding has been suspended shall be 
tolled. 
 
Application 
The rule does not apply to the civil action arising from 
other sources of obligations: law, quasi-delict, quasi-
contract because this can proceed independently of 
criminal action 
1) The independent civil actions in cases under 
Art 32,33, 34 and 2176 of Civil Code because 
you can file this independently, you do not 
have to wait for the criminal action to attain 
finality status. 
2) If the case of separate civil action involves a 
prejudicial question determinative of whether 
the criminal action may proceed or not. 
3) When the civil case is subsequently 
consolidated in the criminal action under this 
section of Rule 111. 
4) When the civil action does not seek to enforce 
civil liability arising from the offenses such as 
civil action for legal separation arising from the 
crime of concubinage 
5) When the civil action is ex contractu and not 
ex delicto, or is based on quasi-delict 
 
Extinction 
GR: The extinction of the penal action does not 
carry with it extinction of the civil action. 
Extinguishment of criminal action 
1) The death of the accused  
2) Dismissal of the criminal action  
XPN: civil action based on delict shall be deemed 
extinguished if there is a finding in a final judgment in 
the criminal action that the act or omission from which 
the civil liability may arise did not exist. 
1) Acquittal of the accused 
 Acquittal on the ground that the 
accused is not the author of the act or 
omission complained of. 
XPN of XPN: 
1) Acquittal is one based on reasonable doubt on 
the guilt of the accused. 
2) Where the court expressly declares that the 
liability of the accused is not criminal but only 
civil in nature.

3) Where the civil liability is not derived from or 
based on the criminal act on which the accused 
is acquitted. 
 
Q: What are the effects of the extinction of civil 
liability to the criminal action? 
A: A judge cannot dismiss a criminal case because the 
civil liability has been paid. While there may be a 
compromise upon civil liability arising from the 
offense, such compromise shall not extinguish the 
public action for the imposition of legal penalty. 
Payment is actually not one of the modes of 
extinguishing criminal liability. 
 
Q: What is the effect on judgement in a civil case 
absolving the defendant to the criminal case? 
A: A final judgement rendered in a civil action 
absolving the defendant from civil liability is not a bar 
to a criminal action for the same act or omission 
subject of the civil action. 
 
Q: What is the effect of the acquittal of the accused to 
his administrative case? 
A: Even if the accused is acquitted in his criminal case, 
it does not absolve him in his administrative case. The 
threshold in admin cases is only substantial evidence or 
a scintilla of proof or reasonable evidence that will 
support a conclusion. 
 
Section 3. When civil action may proceeded independently. 
— In the cases provided for in Articles 32, 33, 34 
and 2176 of the Civil Code of the Philippines, the 
independent civil action may be brought by the 
offended party. It shall proceed independently of the 
criminal action and shall require only a 
preponderance of evidence. In no case, however, 
may the offended party recover damages twice for 
the same act or omission charged in the criminal 
action. (3a) 
 
Independent civil actions 
1) the failure to make a reservation in the criminal 
action, i.e. to file separately any of this action 
provided under Article 32, 33, 34 and 2176, is 
not a waiver of the right to file a separate civil 
action based on this Articles of the Civil Code 
2) civil actions not based on the criminal actions 
3) the grant under Articles 32, 33, 34 and 2176 of 
the Civil Code are substantive in nature, this is 
part of substantive law therefore the right to 
file the same cannot be diminished, increased 
or modified by the Rules of Court without 
offending the Constitution. 
4) an independent civil action separately and 
independently simultaneous with the criminal 
action. That is on top of the civil liability ex 
delicto which may be prosecuted or 
deemed instituted in the criminal action. 
 
Article 32. Any public officer or employee, or any 
private individual, who directly or indirectly 
obstructs, defeats, violates or in any manner impedes 
or impairs any of the following rights and liberties of 
another person shall be liable to the latter for 
damages: 
 
(1) Freedom of religion; 
(2) Freedom of speech; 
(3) Freedom to write for the press or to maintain a 
periodical publication; 
(4) Freedom from arbitrary or illegal detention; 
(5) Freedom of suffrage; 
(6) The right against deprivation of property without 
due process of law; 
(7) The right to a just compensation when private 
property is taken for public use; 
(8) The right to the equal protection of the laws; 
(9) The right to be secure in one's person, house, 
papers, and effects against unreasonable searches 
and seizures; 
(10) The liberty of abode and of changing the same; 
(11) The privacy of communication and 
correspondence; 
(12) The right to become a member of associations 
or societies for purposes not contrary to law; 
(13) The right to take part in a peaceable assembly to 
petition the Government for redress of grievances; 
(14) The right to be a free from involuntary servitude 
in any form; 
(15) The right of the accused against excessive bail; 
(16) The right of the accused to be heard by himself 
and counsel, to be informed of the nature and cause 
of the accusation against him, to have a speedy and 
public trial, to meet the witnesses face to face, and 
to have compulsory process to secure the attendance 
of witness in his behalf; 
(17) Freedom from being compelled to be a witness 
against one's self, or from being forced to confess 
guilt, or from being induced by a promise of 
immunity or reward to make such confession, except 
when the person confessing becomes a State 
witness; 
(18) Freedom from excessive fines, or cruel and 
unusual punishment, unless the same is imposed or 
inflicted in accordance with a statute which has not 
been judicially declared unconstitutional; and 
(19) Freedom of access to the courts. 
 
In any of the cases referred to in this article, whether 
or not the defendant's act or omission constitutes a 
criminal offense, the aggrieved party has a right to 
commence an entirely separate and distinct civil 
action for damages, and for other relief. Such civil 
action shall proceed independently of any criminal 
prosecution (if the latter be instituted), and may be 
proved by a preponderance of evidence. 
 
The indemnity shall include moral damages. 
Exemplary damages may also be adjudicated. 
 
The responsibility herein set forth is not demandable 
from a judge unless his act or omission constitutes a 
violation of the Penal Code or other penal statute. 
 
Article 33. In cases of defamation, fraud, and 
physical injuries a civil action for damages, entirely 
separate and distinct from the criminal action, may 
be brought by the injured party. Such civil action

36 
shall proceed independently of the criminal 
prosecution, and shall require only a preponderance 
of evidence. 
 
Article 34. When a member of a city or municipal 
police force refuses or fails to render aid or 
protection to any person in case of danger to life or 
property, such peace officer shall be primarily liable 
for damages, and the city or municipality shall be 
subsidiarily responsible therefor. The civil action 
herein recognized shall be independent of any 
criminal proceedings, and a preponderance of 
evidence shall suffice to support such action. 
 
Article 2176. Quasi-Delict. Whoever by act or 
omission causes damages to another, there being 
fault or negligence is oblige to pay for the damage 
done. Such fault or negligence is if there is no pre-
existing contractual relation between the parties is 
called quasidelict is governed by the provisions of 
Chapter 2, Title XVII of the Civil Code. 
 
Under here, same act or omission can create two kinds 
of liability on the part of the offender; that is:  
1) civil liability ex delicto such as reckless 
imprudence resulting to homicide and/or 
damage to property; and  
2) liable for quasi-delict or civil liability under Art. 
2176 of Civil Code. 
 
Section 4. Effect of death on civil actions. — The death 
of the accused after arraignment and during the 
pendency of the criminal action shall extinguish the 
civil liability arising from the delict. However, the 
independent civil action instituted under section 3 of 
this Rule or which thereafter is instituted to enforce 
liability arising from other sources of obligation may 
be continued against the estate or legal 
representative of the accused after proper 
substitution or against said estate, as the case may be. 
The heirs of the accused may be substituted for the 
deceased without requiring the appointment of an 
executor or administrator and the court may appoint 
a guardian ad litem for the minor heirs. 
 
The court shall forthwith order said legal 
representative or representatives to appear and be 
substituted within a period of thirty (30) days from 
notice. 
 
A final judgment entered in favor of the offended 
party shall be enforced in the manner especially 
provided in these rules for prosecuting claims 
against the estate of the deceased. 
 
If the accused dies before arraignment, the case shall 
be dismissed without prejudice to any civil action the 
offended party may file against the estate of the 
deceased. (n) 
 
Effects of death to criminal actions 
Art. 89. How criminal liability is totally extinguished. — 
Criminal liability is totally extinguished: 
1) By the death of the convict, as to the personal 
penalties and as to pecuniary penalties, liability 
therefor is extinguished only when the death of 
the offender occurs before final judgment. 
xxx 
 
Effects to civil actions 
 
DEPENDENT ACTIONS 
To civil actions: EXTINGUISHED 
1) Purely person action - The death of a party will 
completely extinguish the claim or the action 
which is purely personal 
2) Civil Actions involving the performance that 
cannot be purely delegated 
3) Claims that cannot be instituted by the 
executor or administrator of the deceased 
person 
 
NOT EXTINGUISHED  
1) Recovery of contractual money, (based on 
contracts)  
2) Recovery or protection of property rights, 
3) Recovery of real and personal property or 
interest,  
4) Enforcement of a lien,  
5) Recovery of damages to injury of a person of 
property and suits by reason of the alleged 
tortuous acts of the defendant, and (torts and 
quasi-delicts)  
6) Ejectment cases 
 
INDEPENDENT CIVIL ACTIONS 
NOT EXTINGUISHED 
1) . It shall be the duty of his counsel to inform 
the court within 30 days after such death of the 
fact thereof and give the names and addresses 
of his legal representative/s 
2) The failure of the counsel to comply is a 
ground for disciplinary action. 
 
1. Death of the accused pending appeal of his 
conviction extinguishes his criminal liability as 
well as the civil liability based solely thereon. As 
opined by Justice Regalado, in this regard, "the death 
of the accused prior to final judgment terminates his 
criminal liability and only the civil liability directly 
arising from and based solely on the offense 
committed, i.e., civil liability ex delicto in senso 
strictiore." 
2. Corollarily, the claim for civil liability survives 
notwithstanding the death of accused, if the same 
may also be predicated on a source of obligation 
other than delict. Article 1157 of the Civil Code 
enumerates these other sources of obligation from 
which the civil liability may arise as a result of the 
same act or omission: 
a) Law  
b) Contracts 
c) Quasi-contracts 
d) . . . 
e) Quasi-delicts
