37 
3. Where the civil liability survives, as explained in 
Number 2 above, an action for recovery therefor 
may be pursued but only by way of filing a 
separate civil action and subject to Section 1, 
Rule 111 of the 1985 Rules on Criminal 
Procedure as amended. This separate civil action 
may be enforced either against the 
executor/administrator or the estate of the accused, 
depending on the source of obligation upon which 
the same is based as explained above. 
4. Finally, the private offended party need not fear a 
forfeiture of his right to file this separate civil action 
by prescription, in cases where during the 
prosecution of the criminal action and prior to its 
extinction, the private-offended party instituted 
together therewith the civil action. In such case, the 
statute of limitations on the civil liability is deemed 
interrupted during the pendency of the criminal case, 
conformably with provisions of Article 1155 21 of 
the Civil Code, that should thereby avoid any 
apprehension on a possible privation of right by 
prescription. 
(People v. Bayotas) 
 
Section 5. Judgment in civil action not a bar. — A final 
judgment rendered in a civil action absolving the 
defendant from civil liability is not a bar to a criminal 
action against the defendant for the same act or 
omission subject of the civil action. (4a) 
 
This applies when the civil action was separately filed 
and decided prior to the institution of the criminal 
action or when the independent civil action that 
proceeded independently with the criminal action was 
decided ahead of the criminal action. 
 
Section 6. Suspension by reason of prejudicial question. — 
A petition for suspension of the criminal action 
based upon the pendency of a prejudicial question in 
a civil action may be filed in the office of the 
prosecutor or the court conducting the preliminary 
investigation. When the criminal action has been 
filed in court for trial, the petition to suspend shall 
be filed in the same criminal action at any time 
before the prosecution rests. (6a) 
 
Section 7. Elements of prejudicial question. — The 
elements of a prejudicial question are: (a) the 
previously instituted civil action involves an issue 
similar or intimately related to the issue raised in the 
subsequent criminal action, and (b) the resolution of 
such issue determines whether or not the criminal 
action may proceed. (5a) 
 
Prejudicial question  
It is a question based on a fact distinct and separate 
from the crime, but so intimately connected with it that 
it determines the guilt or innocence of the accused. It 
is a question which arises in a case, the resolution of 
which is a logical antecedent of the issue involved in 
the said case and the cognizance of which pertains to 
another tribunal 
 
Rationale of prejudicial questions 
The reason behind the principle of a prejudicial 
question is to avoid two conflicting decisions in the 
civil case and in the criminal case. 
 
Applicability 
1) the civil action referred to in issues of 
prejudicial question is not the civil action 
involving the civil liability ex delicto. 
2) it can be law contract or quasi contract, but it 
cannot involve separate civil action under 
Article 32, 33, 34, 2176 or those independent 
civil action because as to those kinds of civil 
action, a prejudicial question cannot arise 
3) based on other sources of obligation such as 
contract, quasi-contract or law 
4) If both are civil cases, then there is no 
prejudicial question. 
5) If both are criminal cases, then again, no 
prejudicial question. 
6) If both are administrative cases or one is 
administrative while the other is criminal, then 
there is no prejudicial question. 
 
Q: Who files the petition for suspension in the criminal 
case when there is prejudicial question? 
A: The rule is actually silent on who should file the 
petition for suspension. It is admitted that the accused 
or even the prosecution can file a petition to suspend 
based on prejudicial question. 
 
Q: Where do you file a petition to suspend a criminal 
action based on prejudicial question? 
A:  
1) When the criminal action is still pending 
preliminary investigation with the office of 
the prosecutor, then you can file for 
suspension in the proper office where the 
officer conducting the preliminary 
investigation is. 
2) If directly filed in court, prejudicial question is 
filed it in the court where the criminal action is 
spending 
 
The previously instituted civil action in this case is a 
declaration of nullity of marriage. Subsequently, a 
criminal action for frustrated parricide was filed. In 
that criminal action, there was a motion to suspend 
criminal proceedings because of alleged prejudicial 
question. The SC said that at the time of the 
commission of the alleged crime, petitioner and 
respondent were married. The subsequent 
dissolution of their marriage in the previously 
instituted civil action will have no effect on the 
alleged crime that was committed at the time of 
the subsistence of their marriage. (Pimentel v. Pimentel) 
 
There are 2 separately instituted civil actions in this 
case. 1) For sum of money; 2) The cancellation of 
mortgage, delivery of title, and damages. The issues 
in these previously instituted civil action is one 
whether a loan was obtained and whether a 

--- PAGE 38 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
38 
mortgage was executed in relation to that loan. The 
subject matter of the subsequently filed criminal 
action of perjury was lying in an affidavit of loss to 
support the request for issuance of a new owner’s 
duplicate copy of the certificate of title. There was 
no prejudicial question (Magestrado v. People) 
 
The previously filed civil action in the case of Yap 
vs. Cabales was a civil action for collection of a sum 
of money. Therefore, the issue in a collection suit for 
sum of money - whether or not the defendant is 
liable to pay the plaintiff a sum of money. It does not 
answer whether or not the accused in a BP 22 case 
issued a bad check. (Yap v. Cabales) 
 
The previously instituted civil action there was 
declaration of nullity of interest of five percent per 
day. The offender issued a check and eventually it 
bounced. Therefore, there is a criminal action for BP 
22. the moment that he signed a check, the check 
issued to the plaintiff and deposited the check to the 
bank and the bank eventually dishonored it, that 
already consummates BP 22. (Jose v. Suarez) 
 
In the previously instituted civil action, there was an 
issue whether or not novation took place in the 
obligation of the parties in the civil case. The 
determination whether there was a novation again 
will not affect the determination of whether or not 
there was an issuance of a bad check – a check issued 
and there after dishonored. Thus, there is no 
prejudicial question in this case. (Landbank v. 
Jacinto) 
 
The previously instituted civil action in this case is 
the declaration of nullity of the appointments of the 
corporate officers then the subsequent criminal 
action that was filed was two counts of Estafa for 
failure to return company cars entrusted to a 
previous officer. The old officers then filed for 
suspension of the criminal action based on 
prejudicial question. They argued that in the 
previously instituted civil action there is an issue of 
whether or not the officers are duly installed or duly 
elected. In that sense, the issue to be determined in 
the previously filed civil action is actually 
determinative of the guilt or innocence of the 
accused in the criminal action. Therefore, there is a 
prejudicial question. (Omictin v. CA) 
 
The previously instituted civil action is also for the 
determination of the validity of the election of the 
new officers and the subsequently filed criminal 
action is again for Estafa and one of the essential 
elements is abuse of confidence. Now, the 
determination whether there was abused of 
confidence in the subsequently file criminal action 
also rests into whether or not an officer was validly 
elected in the corporation. Therefore, the 
determination in the previously instituted civil action 
as to the validity of the election of the new set of 
officers has a bearing or is determinative of whether 
or not the element of Estafa in the subsequently file 
criminal action is present or not. Therefore, there is 
a prejudicial question. (People v. Arambulo) 
 
The previously instituted civil case is an 
administrative case for the specific performance 
before the Housing Land Use Regulatory Board. 
Subsequently, a criminal action for violation of PD 
957 for non-delivery of titles in violation of the 
applicable provisions of PD 957. It is essentially of 
the same issue but the modes of enforcing the 
actions were different. One is in an administrative 
body and the other is a criminal action in court. The 
Supreme Court said that it is a prejudicial question – 
the specific performance case filed in the HULRB 
and therefore the subsequently filed criminal action 
based on the violation of PD 957 should be 
suspended. (San Miguel Properties v. Perez) 
 
 
PRELIMINARY INVESTIGATION 
 
RULE 112 
Section 1. Preliminary investigation defined; when required. 
— Preliminary investigation is an inquiry or 
proceeding to determine whether there is sufficient 
ground to engender a well-founded belief that a 
crime has been committed and the respondent is 
probably guilty thereof, and should be held for trial. 
 
Except as provided in section 7 of this Rule, a 
preliminary investigation is required to be conducted 
before the filing of a complaint or information for 
an offense where the penalty prescribed by law is at 
least four (4) years, two (2) months and one (1) day 
without regard to the fine. (1a) 
 
DEFINITION OF “PRELIMINARY 
INVESTIGATION”  
The inquiry or proceeding, that is the preliminary 
investigation, is to determine whether there is sufficient 
ground to engender a well-founded belief. This is what 
we call the probable cause in preliminary investigation, 
which we will define later on. And the probable cause, 
is that there is a crime that has been committed and 
that the crime committed is committed by the 
respondent, who is probably guilty thereof and should 
be held for trial in a judicial criminal proceeding.  
 
NATURE 
Executive function 
Function that belongs to the public prosecutor 
Substantive rights 
A personal right and can be waived expressly or 
impliedly 
PURPOSES OF PRELIMINARY 
INVESTIGATION  
A. To inquire concerning the commission of a 
crime and the connection of the accused with 
it, in order that he may be informed of the 
nature and character of the crime charged 
against him, and if there is probable cause, for 
believing him guilty that the state shall take the 
necessary steps to bring him to trial;  

--- PAGE 39 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
39 
B. To preserve the evidence and keep the 
witnesses within the control of the state and to 
determine the amount of bail if the offense is 
bailable 
Probable cause pertains to the facts and 
circumstances sufficient to support a well-founded 
belief that a crime has been committed and the accused 
is probably guilty thereof 
RIGHT OF PRELIMINARY INVESTIGATION 
1) Not a constitutional right 
2) Waivable 
PUBLIC PROSECUTOR 
1) The preliminary investigation by a public 
prosecutor is not the stage to decide whether there 
is evidence beyond reasonable doubt of the guilt of 
the person charged. 
2) The main function of the government prosecutor 
during the preliminary investigation is merely to 
determine the existence of probable cause and to 
file the corresponding information if he finds it to 
be so. 
3) if there is no probable cause, not enough evidence 
to support the probability of guilt, then it is the 
duty of an investigating prosecutor to dismiss the 
case to protect an innocent from a hasty, expensive 
and useless criminal trial. 
 
KINDS OF DETERMINATION OF 
PROBABLE CAUSE 
Preliminary 
Investigation 
Preliminary 
Examination/Inquiry

probable cause by the 
Executive Department 
determination of

Executive function Judicial function 
 
JURISDICTION OF COURTS 
GR: Courts are precluded from reviewing findings of 
the prosecutors – findings of probable cause. The 
rationale behind the rule rests on the principle of 
separation of powers since the determination of 
probable cause, in relation to preliminary investigation 
is an executive function. 
 
XPN: When there is a clear grave abuse of discretion 
amounting to lack or excess of jurisdiction. The abuse 
must be shown to be so patent, so gross as to amount 
to an invasion of a positive duty or a virtual refusal to 
perform a duty enjoined by law, or to act at all in 
contemplation of law as where the power is exercised 
in an arbitrary and despotic manner by reason of 
passion or hostility.

What are the offenses requiring preliminary 
investigation?  
Under paragraph 2 of Section 1, except when the 
accused was lawfully arrested without a warrant, a 
preliminary investigation is required to be conducted 
before the filing of the complaint or information for an 
offense when the penalty prescribed by law is at least 4 
years, 2 months and 1 day without regard to fine. 
 
a public prosecutor needs to look at the prescribed 
penalty, which is the penalty indicated by the law for 
the crime and not the imposable penalty which refers 
to that penalty to be imposed on the accused after 
considering the evidence and attendant modifying 
circumstances in the case. 
 
Preliminary investigation not required 
1) When the accused was lawfully arrested without a 
warrant and he does not ask for a preliminary 
investigation, this is called an inquest case.  
2) When the penalty prescribed for the offense charged 
does not exceed 4 years 2 months (and 1 day?), the 
cases falling under this category has been called as 
direct filing cases. 
 
What is the effect of a lack of preliminary 
investigation?  
Consistently, the Supreme Court has held that the 
absence of a preliminary investigation  
1) does not impair the validity of the criminal 
information or render it defective 
2) It does not also affect the jurisdiction of the 
trial court 

--- PAGE 40 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
40 
3) But if there is no preliminary investigation and 
the accused before entering his plea invites the 
attention of the court to its absence (when the 
lack of preliminary investigation is 
appropriately and timely raised by the accused 
meaning), the court, instead of dismissing the 
case, should remand the case to the prosecutor 
so that a preliminary investigation may be 
conducted. 
4) Not a ground to motion to quash 
5) Waivable - if the accused fails to raise the lack 
of preliminary investigation during his or her 
arraignment and he already took his plea 
Instances when probable cause needs to be 
established 
1) Under Sections 1 and 3 of the Rule 112 in 
Preliminary Investigation by the 
investigating officer to determine whether 
there is sufficient ground to engender a well-
founded belief that a crime has been 
committed, and the respondent is probably 
guilty thereof and should be held for trial. A 
preliminary investigation is required before the 
filing of a complaint or information for an 
offense when the penalty prescribed by law is 
at least 4 years, 2 months and 1 day without 
regard to fine. 
2) Under Sections 5 and 8 of Rule 112, by the 
judge to determine whether a warrant of 
arrest or a commitment order, if the accused 
has already been arrested, shall be issued and 
that there is a necessity of placing the 
respondent under the immediate custody in 
order not to frustrate the ends of justice. 
3) Under Section 5 (b) of Rule 113, by a peace 
officer or a private person making a 
warrantless arrest when an offense has just 
been committed and he has probable cause to 
believe, based on personal knowledge of facts 
or circumstances that the person to be arrested 
has committed it. 
4) Under Section 4 of Rule 126, on search and 
seizure by a judge to determine whether a 
search warrant shall be issued and only upon 
probable cause in connection with one specific 
offense to be determined personally by the 
judge after examination under oath or 
affirmation of the complainant and the 
witnesses he may produce and particularly 
describing the place to be searched and the 
things to be seized, which may be anywhere in 
the Philippines. 
Revised Manual for Prosecutors 2017 Edition  
Section 4.1.2. Quantum of Evidence Required is Probable 
Cause. — Probable cause has been defined as a 
reasonable ground of presumption that a matter is or 
may be well-founded; such a state of facts in the mind 
of the prosecutor as would lead a person of ordinary 
caution and prudence to believe, or entertain an honest 
or strong suspicion, that a thing is so.  
The term does not mean “actual and positive cause” 
nor does it import absolute certainty. It is merely based 
on opinion and reasonable belief. Thus, a finding of 
probable cause does not require an inquiry into 
whether there is sufficient evidence to procure a 
conviction. It is enough that it is believed that an act or 
omission complained of constitutes the offense 
charged. Precisely, there is a trial for the reception of 
evidence of the prosecution in support of the charge.  
Probable cause, for purposes of filing a criminal 
information, has been defined as such facts as are 
sufficient to engender a well-founded belief that a 
crime has been committed and that respondent is 
probably guilty thereof, and should be held for trial.  
Probable cause is meant such set of facts and 
circumstances which would lead a reasonably discreet 
and prudent man to believe that the offense charged in 
the Information, or any offense included therein, has 
been committed by the person sought to be arrested.  
In determining probable cause, the average person 
weighs facts and circumstances without resorting to 
the calibrations of the rules of evidence of which 
he/she has no technical knowledge. He/She relies on 
common sense.  
A finding of probable cause needs only to rest on 
evidence showing that, more likely than not, a crime 
has been committed and that it was committed by the 
accused.  
Probable cause demands more than bare suspicion, but 
it requires less than evidence that would justify a 
conviction.  
What is determined during preliminary investigation is 
only probable cause, not proof beyond reasonable 
doubt. As implied by the words themselves, probable 
cause is concerned with probability, not absolute or 
moral certainty.  
A finding of probable cause does not require an inquiry 
into whether there is sufficient evidence to secure a 
conviction. It is enough that the act or omission 
complained of constitutes the offense charged. The 
term does not mean “actual and positive cause” nor 
does it import absolute certainty. It is merely based on 
opinion and reasonable belief. 
Section 4.1.3 
Section 4.1.3. No Fixed Formula for Determining 
Probable Cause. – The determination of probable 
cause does not call for the application of rules and 
standards of proof that a judgment of conviction 
requires after trial on the merits. As implied by the 
words themselves, “probable cause” is concerned with 
probability, not absolute or even moral certainty. The 

--- PAGE 41 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
41 
prosecution need not present at this stage proof 
beyond reasonable doubt. The standards of judgment 
are those of a reasonably prudent man, not the exacting 
calibrations of a judge after a full-blown trial. No law 
or rule states that probable cause requires a specific 
kind of evidence. No formula or fixed rule for its 
determination exists. Probable cause is determined in 
the light of conditions obtaining in a given situation. 
Section 4.1.4. Sound Discretion of the Prosecutor. – 
The institution of a criminal action depends upon the 
sound discretion of the prosecutor. He may or may not 
file the complaint or information, follow or not follow 
that presented by the offended party, according to 
whether the evidence, in his opinion, is sufficient or 
not to establish the guilt of the accused beyond 
reasonable doubt. The reason for placing the criminal 
prosecution under the direction and control of the 
[prosecutor] is to prevent malicious or unfounded 
prosecution by private persons. x x x Prosecuting 
officers under the power vested in them by law, not 
only have the authority but also the duty of prosecuting 
persons who, according to the evidence received from 
the complainant, are shown to be guilty of a crime 
committed within the jurisdiction of their office. They 
have equally the legal duty not to prosecute when after 
an investigation they become convinced that the 
evidence adduced is not sufficient to establish a prima 
facie case. 
Section 4.2. Purposes of a Preliminary Investigation. –  
a. To secure the innocent against hasty, malicious and 
oppressive prosecution and to protect him/her from 
an open and public accusation of a crime and from the 
trouble, expense and anxiety of a public trial; and  
b. To protect the State from having to conduct useless 
and expensive trials.  
The primary objective of a Preliminary Investigation is 
to free the respondent from the inconvenience, 
expense, ignominy and stress of defending 
himself/herself in the course of a formal trial, until the 
reasonable probability of his/her guilt in a more or less 
summary proceeding by a competent office designated 
by law for that purpose has been determined.  
Secondarily, such summary proceeding also protects 
the State from the burden of the unnecessary expense 
and effort in prosecuting alleged offenses, and in 
holding trials arising from false, frivolous or groundless 
charges. 
Section 4.2.1. Double Jeopardy in Preliminary 
Investigation. – The dismissal of a case during 
preliminary investigation does not constitute double 
jeopardy, preliminary investigation not being part of 
the trial. 
 
the requisites for double jeopardy to attach is that 
there is:  
1. A first jeopardy must have attached prior to the 
second one;  
2. The first jeopardy must have been validly 
terminated;  
3. The second jeopardy must be for the same offense 
as the first jeopardy 
Legal jeopardy attaches only upon:  
1. Valid indictment;  
2. Before a competent court;  
3. After arraignment;  
4. A valid plea having been entered; and  
5. The case was dismissed or otherwise terminated 
without the express consent of the accused. 
Section 4.3. Nature of a Preliminary Investigation. – 
4.3.1. It is an Executive Function. – A preliminary 
investigation is not a quasi-judicial proceeding. [The 
investigating prosecutor] does not exercise 
adjudication nor rule–making functions. It is a function 
of the Office of the National Prosecution Service 
which is under the control and supervision of the 
Department of Justice within the executive branch of 
the government. 
4.3.2. It is Preliminary in Nature. – The investigation is 
advisedly called preliminary, as it is yet to be followed 
by the trial proper. The investigating officer acts upon 
probable cause and reasonable belief, not proof 
beyond reasonable doubt 
4.3.3. It is a Summary and Inquisitorial Proceeding. – 
While it is a summary proceeding, it is done in a 
scrupulous manner to prevent material damage to 
a potential accused’s constitutional right to liberty 
and the guarantees of freedom and fair play. Merely 
inquisitorial; not trial on the merits; It must be stressed 
that the right of an accused to a preliminary 
investigation is a personal right and can be waived 
expressly or by implication; if failed to invoke 
before entering a plea; it is not part of the due 
process guaranteed by the Constitution; and does 
not place the respondent in jeopardy 
 
4.3.4. It is not a Judicial Inquiry or Proceeding. – 
Preliminary investigation is merely inquisitorial, and 
is often the only means of discovering the persons who 
may be reasonably charged with a crime and to enable 
the fiscal to prepare his/ her complaint or information. 
It is not a trial of the case on the merits and has no 
purpose except that of determining whether a crime 
has been committed and whether there is probable 
cause to believe that the accused is guilty thereof. While 

--- PAGE 42 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
42 
the fiscal makes that determination, he/she cannot be 
said to be acting as a quasi- court, for it is the courts, 
ultimately, that pass judgment on the accused, not the 
fiscal. 
Section 4.4. Right to Preliminary Investigation. – 4.4.1. 
It is not a Constitutional Right. – The right to a 
preliminary investigation is not a constitutional right, 
meaning, it is not expressly provided for in the 
Constitution. It is not therefore guaranteed by the 
Constitution unlike for instance, the right to counsel or 
to remain silent which is expressly embodied under 
Section 12 of Article III of the Constitution. 
4.4.2. It is Merely a Statutory Grant. – The right to a 
preliminary investigation is statutory, not 
constitutional.” In short, the rights of a respondent in 
a preliminary investigation are merely statutory rights, 
not constitutional due process rights. 
4.4.3. It is a Personal Right. – The right to a 
preliminary investigation is also a personal right, 
which can be waived expressly or impliedly. 
Preliminary investigation is not part of the due process 
guaranteed by the Constitution. It is an inquiry to 
determine whether there is sufficient ground to 
engender a well-founded belief that a crime has been 
committed and the respondent is probably guilty 
thereof. Instead, the right to a preliminary investigation 
is personal. It is afforded to the accused by statute, and 
can be waived, either expressly or by implication. The 
waiver extends to any irregularity in the preliminary 
investigation, where one was conducted.  
In inquest cases, the accused impliedly waives his/her 
right to a preliminary investigation, if he/she fails to 
invoke the same within five (5) days from the time 
he/she learns of the filing of the Information. This 
five-day period for the filing of a motion for 
preliminary investigation after an Information has been 
filed in court against an accused who was arrested 
without a warrant, has been characterized as 
mandatory. 
4.4.4. It is a Substantive Right. – The right to 
preliminary investigation is substantive, not merely 
formal or technical. To deny it to petitioner would 
deprive him/her of the full measure of his/her right to 
due process. 
 
Section 2. Officers authorized to conduct preliminary 
investigations. — 
 
The following may conduct preliminary 
investigations: 
 
(a) Provincial or City Prosecutors and their 
assistants; 
(b) Judges of the Municipal Trial Courts and 
Municipal Circuit Trial Courts; 
(c) National and Regional State Prosecutors; and 
(d) Other officers as may be authorized by law. 
 
Their authority to conduct preliminary 
investigations shall include all crimes cognizable by 
the proper court in their respective territorial 
jurisdictions. (2a) 
 
The Supreme Court has withdrawn the authority of 
judges of the municipal trial court or the first level 
courts to conduct preliminary investigation

Section 3. Procedure. — The preliminary 
investigation shall be conducted in the following 
manner: 
 
(a) The complaint shall state the address of the 
respondent and shall be accompanied by the 
affidavits of the complainant and his witnesses, as 
well as other supporting documents to establish 
probable cause. They shall be in such number of 
copies as there are respondents, plus two (2) copies 
for the official file. The affidavits shall be subscribed 
and sworn to before any prosecutor or government 
official authorized to administer oath, or, in their 
absence or unavailability, before a notary public, 
each of who must certify that he personally 
examined the affiants and that he is satisfied that 
they voluntarily executed and understood their 
affidavits. 
 
(b) Within ten (10) days after the filing of the 
complaint, the investigating officer shall either 
dismiss it if he finds no ground to continue with the 
investigation, or issue a subpoena to the respondent 
attaching to it a copy of the complaint and its 
supporting affidavits and documents. 
 
The respondent shall have the right to examine the 
evidence submitted by the complainant which he 
may not have been furnished and to copy them at 
his expense. If the evidence is voluminous, the 
complainant may be required to specify those which 
he intends to present against the respondent, and 
these shall be made available for examination or 
copying by the respondent at his expense. 
 
Objects as evidence need not be furnished a party 
but shall be made available for examination, copying, 
or photographing at the expense of the requesting 
party. 
 
(c) Within ten (10) days from receipt of the 
subpoena with the complaint and supporting 
affidavits and documents, the respondent shall 
submit his counter-affidavit and that of his witnesses 
and other supporting documents relied upon for his 
defense. The counter-affidavits shall be subscribed 
and sworn to and certified as provided in paragraph 
(a) of this section, with copies thereof furnished by 
him to the complainant. The respondent shall not be 
allowed to file a motion to dismiss in lieu of a 
counter-affidavit. 
 
(d) If the respondent cannot be subpoenaed, or if 
subpoenaed, does not submit counter-affidavits 
within the ten (10) day period, the investigating 
officer shall resolve the complaint based on the 
evidence presented by the complainant. 
 
(e) The investigating officer may set a hearing if there 
are facts and issues to be clarified from a party or a 
witness. The parties can be present at the hearing but 
without the right to examine or cross-examine. They 
may, however, submit to the investigating officer 
questions which may be asked to the party or witness 
concerned. 
 
The hearing shall be held within ten (10) days from 
submission of the counter-affidavits and other 
documents or from the expiration of the period for 
their submission. It shall be terminated within five 
(5) days. 
 
(f) Within ten (10) days after the investigation, the 
investigating officer shall determine whether or not 
there is sufficient ground to hold the respondent for 
trial. (3a) 
 
1) submission of the complaint with affidavits 
and supporting documents, and then  
2) the submission of counter-affidavits with 
supporting affidavits and documents 
3) If it is necessary, the investigating officer may 
set a hearing to conduct clarificatory 
questioning from both parties. But during this 
clarificatory hearing, there is no right to 
examine or cross-examine. The only right given 
here is to submit to the investigating officers 
questions which either party would like to ask 
to the witnesses or to the opposing party. 
4) After the clarificatory questioning (which 
under the rules should be terminated within 
five days), then the end of the preliminary 
investigation will be the last day of the 
clarificatory hearing. And the investigating 
prosecutor is given ten (10) days to decide 
whether there is probable cause or none. 
However, if there is no clarificatory hearing, 
the investigation will end at the submission of 
the counter-affidavit. 
5) reply to the Counter-Affidavit. If the other 
party wants to reply to the Reply-Affidavit, 
they can file a Rejoinder Affidavit. 
 
Affidavits, counter-affidavits and supporting 
documents 
1) should be made on personal knowledge 
2) should show affirmatively that the affiants are 
competent to testify on the matters stated 
therein. 
3) sworn to before any prosecutor or government 
official authorized to administer oath, or only 
in their absence and unavailability, a notary 
public who must certify that he personally 
examined the affiants and that he is satisfied 
that they voluntarily executed and understood 
their affidavits. 
 Affidavits, notarized by a notary public 
without any showing that a prosecutor 
or qualified government official was 
unavailable, are not in accord with the 
rules. Thus, it may not be considered or 
accepted by the investigating 
prosecutor.

Upon filing of the complaint 
1. Dismiss the complaint — The investigating 
prosecutor is empowered or authorized to actually 
dismiss the complaint within ten (10) days after its 
filing if he finds no ground to continue with the 
investigation; or  
2. Issue a subpoena — He or she may issue a subpoena 
to a respondent attaching to it the copy of the 
complaint and its supporting affidavits and documents 
and directing the respondent to file a counter-affidavit 
 
Sec 3c 
A verified motion to dismiss may be filed on the 
following grounds: 
1) The absence of probable cause;  
2) The fact that the complaint or one similar 
thereto or identical therewith has previously 
been filed with the office and has been fully 
adjudicated upon the merits after due 
preliminary investigation proceeding; and  
3) The extinction of the respondent's criminal 
liability by reason of death, pardon, amnesty, 
repeal of the law under which the prosecution 
is sought, or any other legal causes such as for 
example the lack of jurisdiction of the 
investigating prosecutor. 
 
Rights of the complainants and respondents under Sec. 
3, Rule 112  
1. Respondents — has the right to receive a copy 
of the complaint and its supporting affidavits 
and documents.  
 Respondents and co-respondents have 
no right to demand that they be 
furnished with copies of the counter-
affidavits of their co-respondents. 
 
what the respondents right to examine refers only to 
the evidence submitted by the complainant and not 
to the counter-affidavits of his co-respondents 
(Jinggoy Estrada vs. OMB) 
 
2. 2. Complainant — has the right to receive a 
copy of the counter-affidavits and the 
affidavits and supporting documents from the 
respondent. 
 
Sec 3d 
Remember, preliminary investigation can actually 
proceed ex-parte or without the participation of the 
respondents. It is under Section 3(d), Rule 112. The 
rule allows the investigating prosecutor to resolve the 
complaint based on the evidence before him if the 
respondent could not be made to participate in the 
preliminary investigation despite the efforts to reach 
him. However, it must be shown that the investigating 
prosecutor, at the very least, had the genuine effort to 
reach the respondent or to furnish him with the 
complaint and the affidavits and the documents 
supporting such complaint 
 
during the preliminary investigation, all the other 
respondents were given a subpoena and a copy of 
the complaint and the supporting documents. 
However, Mr. Labay was not furnished the 
subpoena, the complaint, the affidavits, and the 
supporting documents because two of the addresses 
to which the subpoena, etc. were sent to were his 
previous office address and his previous residence. 
Therefore, despite several attempts, the subpoena 
could really not served upon Mr. Labay. Because of 
this failure of service, Mr. Labay became unaware 
that there was already a preliminary investigation 
against him. His daughter, Atty. Labay, only found 
out that there was an investigation and that there was 
already a resolution because Atty. Labay saw it on 
TV. she requested for copies of the complaint and 
the supporting documents. However, the request 
was denied. Instead she was given a copy of the 
resolution approving the filing of the information 
against, among others, respondent Labay for 
violation of Anti-Graft and Corrupt Practices Act 
before the Sandiganbayan. A first Omnibus motion 
for re-investigation was then filed and a copy of the 
complaint and the supporting documents were 
requested. This was denied by the Ombudsman, 
thus, a motion for reconsideration was filed which 
was denied again. 
 
Subsequently, the information was filed before the 
Sandiganbayan and it was only days after the filing 
of the information that Mr. Labay was given a copy 
of the complaint along with the copy of the affidavits 
and documents, but that happened only after the 
information was filed before the Sandiganbayan.  
 
However, the peculiar circumstance here is Mr. 
Labay did not want to evade the service of the 
subpoena. He didn’t know but upon knowing or 
gaining knowledge, he was the one who volunteered 
to have a copy of the complaint and the supporting 
documents in order for him to properly answer. Mr. 
Labay really didn’t have any attempt to delay the 
prosecution of this offense he had genuinely wanted 
to participate, but that the Ombudsman did not give 
him that opportunity by not giving him timely the 
copy of the complaint and the supporting 
documents.  
 
Actually, the Ombudsman could have properly 
resolve the preliminary investigation even without 
the participation of Mr. Labay because that is under 
the rules. However, because it was Mr. Labay 
himself who volunteered, who approached the 
Ombudsman to participate to have a copy of the 
complaint and supporting documents in order for 
him to properly answer, properly present his 
defense, but despite that effort the Ombudsman still 
denied Mr. Labay’s request to be furnished timely a 
copy of the complaint and the supporting 
documents. 
 
The Supreme Court said that while the Ombudsman 
was correct in resolving the complaint based on the 
evidence presented in accordance with the rules even 
if no counter-affidavit was filed by Mr. Labay, there’s 

--- PAGE 45 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
45 
no dispute that the Ombudsman was unable to serve 
copies of the complaint or the supporting 
documents prior to or even during the preliminary 
investigation of the case meaning.  
 
The Supreme Court noted however, that the 
situation effectively changed when petitioner made 
himself available to the Ombudsman when he 
requested access to the case records. The 
Ombudsman had a clear opportunity to furnish 
petitioner with copies of the complaint affidavit and 
its supporting documents. Instead, it merely decided 
to furnish petitioner with a copy of its resolution.  
 
The Supreme Court added that even assuming that 
the Ombudsman was merely complying with Atty. 
Labay's requests for information when it responded 
with case titles and docket numbers of the cases 
pending against petitioner Labay, it should have 
exercised its duty to inform the petitioner of the 
charges filed against him by furnishing him copies of 
the complaint affidavit and its supporting 
documents, or at least, it should have directed and 
allowed petitioner to access the records, at its office 
pursuant to the right of the respondent to examine 
the evidence submitted by the complainant. But this 
was not done by the Ombudsman.  
 
by the mere fact that the petitioner, Atty. Labay was 
not yet furnished a copy of the complaint affidavit 
and the supporting documents at the time that the 
Ombudsman furnished him a copy of the resolution 
finding probable cause against him, it is clear that he 
could not effectively and sufficiently address the 
allegation against him. Atty. Labay should not be 
blamed for being unable to raise any substantive 
defense in either the omnibus motions he filed with 
the Ombudsman, since he had not even seen any of 
the allegations filed against him by the Ombudsman. 
More importantly, he could not have been expected 
to seek appropriate evidence to support his defense 
when he wasn't even given any access to the 
documents submitted by the Sandiganbayan in 
support of its complaint.  
 
The Supreme Court further added that in fact, the 
violation of petitioner Labay's constitutional right to 
due process is made even more evident when the 
Ombudsman unceremoniously denied his request to 
be furnished copies of the complaint affidavit and its 
supporting documents in the first Omnibus motion 
and reiterated in his second Omnibus motion in 
both orders denying the two Omnibus motions. The 
Ombudsman seemingly ignored petitioners request 
and effectively denied petitioner of his right to 
secure copies of the complaint affidavit. 
 
Unfortunately, the Sandiganbayan committed grave 
abuse of discretion when it failed to grant petition 
Labay's extremely urgent Omnibus motion, despite 
the glaring violation committed by the Ombudsman. 
The Sandiganbayan should have recognized these 
patent violations and ordered the remand of the case 
to the Ombudsman for the conduct of a proper 
preliminary investigation with respect to petitioner 
Labay's participation in the crimes charged.  
 
There is also no merit in the Ombudsman’s position, 
that it was incumbent on petitioner Labay to justify 
his whereabouts during the time that the 
Ombudsman was attempting service of the 
subpoena on him, since no law or regulation requires 
an accused in a preliminary investigation to submit 
himself to the Ombudsman, or at the very least, to 
update the latter of his latest address (Labay v. Sandiganbayan) 
 
GR: the preliminary investigation can go on ex parte 
without the participation of the respondent if he was 
already served a subpoena and he fails or refuses to 
submit counter affidavit within the 10 days provided 
for under the rules, that will not constrain the 
investigating prosecutor to proceed with the 
preliminary investigation in finding whether or not 
there is probably a cause. Even just based on the 
complaint, and the supporting affidavits and 
documents provided for by the complainant 
 
XPN: when circumstances like in the case of Labay v. 
Sandiganbayan happens, where it is actually the 
respondent who is willing and volunteering to 
participate in the preliminary investigation, but still, the 
investigating prosecutor denies the rights of the 
responded under the rules, then it would be considered 
as grave abuse of discretion. 
 
Sec 3e – CLARIFICATORY HEARING 
the investigating officer has the right to set a hearing 
for a clarificatory question. This is discretionary for the 
investigating officer and not mandatory at all. 
Therefore, since it is discretionary, the absence of a 
clarificatory hearing is not a denial of due process. 
 
Under DOJ Circular No. 01-2000, all cases of 
preliminary investigation being conducted by 
investigating prosecutors shall be completely 
disposed of within a period of 60 days from the 
date of assignment. This period shall include hearings 
conducted in preparation and issuance of resolutions 
and corresponding information if any. 
 
However, the noncompliance with the period does not 
affect the validity of the resolution or the information. 
It will be the investigating prosecutor who will just be 
subject to disciplinary action if there is no justifiable 
explanation of why there is a delay in the resolution of 
the preliminary investigation. 
 
Sec 3f 
Officially the termination of the preliminary 
investigation is upon the filing of the information in 
the proper court or when there is a dismissal of the 
complaint, the approval of the proper approving 
authority. So for preliminary investigation conducted 
by the:  
 Provincial Prosecutor's Office, it will 
be the provincial prosecutor who will 
approve.

 City Prosecutor's Office, it will be the

 Regional State Prosecutor's office, it 
will be the Chief State Prosecutor or

 In cases of offenses cognizable by the 
Sandiganbayan in the exercise of its 
original jurisdiction, it will be the

deputies will be from Luzon, Visayas, 
Mindanao, and from the military 
 
Certification of preliminary investigation 
If he finds in the resolution that there is probable 
cause, the information shall contain a certification by 
the investigating officer under oath in which he shall 
certify to the following:  
a) that he or as shown by the record, an 
authorized officer, has personally examined the 
complainant and his witnesses;  
b) that there is reasonable ground to believe that 
a crime has been committed;  
c) that the accused is probably guilty thereof;  
d) that the accused was informed of the complaint 
and of the evidence submitted against him; and 
e) that he was given an opportunity to submit 
controverting evidence 
 
Effect of absence 
Notwithstanding the absence in the information of a 
certification as to the holding of a preliminary 
investigation, the information is nonetheless 
considered valid for the reason that such certification 
is not an essential part of the information itself, and its 
absence cannot vitiate the information. 
 
What is not allowed is the filing of the information 
without a preliminary investigation, having been 
previously conducted in those instances where 
preliminary investigation is necessary. 
 
within five days from the resolution, the investigating 
prosecutor shall forward the record of the case to the 
proper approving authority. The approving authority 
shall act on the resolution within 10 days from the 
receipt thereof and shall immediately inform the parties 
of such action. 
 
Forwarding of the records for action 
no complaint or information may be filed or dismissed 
by the investigating prosecutor without the prior 
written authority or approval of the corresponding 
approving authority. Thus, the resolution of the 
investigating prosecutor may be reversed or affirmed 
by the Provincial or City Prosecutor or the Prosecutor 
General or the Ombudsman as the case may be. 
 
Rule when recommendation for dismissal is 
disapproved 
Where the investigating prosecutor recommends the 
dismissal of the complaint, but his recommendation is 
disapproved by the Provincial or City Prosecutor or the 
Chief Prosecutor or the Ombudsman or his deputy on 
the ground that probable cause exists, the latter may  
1) by himself file the information against the 
respondent or  
2) direct another assistant prosecutor or state 
prosecutor to do so without conducting 
another preliminary investigation. (it will no 
longer be the investigating prosecutor who will 
handle the case and file the information in 
court. It will be a totally different prosecutor.) 
 
Motion for reconsideration 
Under the rules of the Department of Justice, the  
 
1) aggrieved party under the preliminary 
investigation may actually file a motion for 
reconsideration within 15 days from the receipt 
of the assailed resolution. 
2) If the motion is denied, the aggrieved party 
may appeal within 15 days from the denial of 
the motion for reconsideration and the appeal 
is taken to the Office of the Secretary of 
Justice. 
 
It will be termed a petition for review before the 
Secretary of Justice who has the power to reverse, 
modify or affirm the prosecutor’s determination in 
preliminary investigation. 
 
Appeals 
Secretary of Justice 
The appeal to the Secretary of justice, however, does 
not hold or prevent the filing of a corresponding 
information in court based on the finding of probable 
cause in the appealed resolution unless the secretary of 
justice directs otherwise, but the one appealing and the 
prosecutor shall see to it that pending resolution of the 
appeal, the proceedings in court are held in abeyance. 
 
The party filing a petition for review is allowed to file 
a motion for the suspension of the arraignment and 
upon motion by the proper party the arraignment shall 
be suspended, among others, if a petitioned for review 
of the resolution of the prosecutor is pending. 
 
The authority of the investigating prosecutor and even 
the Secretary of the Department of Justice as 
previously discussed, is a discretion that is executive in 
nature but if there is grave abuse of discretion, then the 
court can step in and nullify or reverse the act which 
was done in grave abuse of discretion. 
 
Office of the President 
The resolution of the Secretary of Justice in appealed 
cases, may either be 
1)  brought to the Court of Appeals via petition 
for certiorari under Rule 65 of the Rules of 
Court based on grave abuse of discretion or  
2) it may be brought on appeal to the Office of 
the President ONLY in the following cases 

--- PAGE 47 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
47 
a) the offense involved is punishable by 
reclusion perpetua to death;  
 For offenses which involve 
imprisonment, which is lower than 
reclusion perpetua to death, the only 
mode of raising it up would be 
judicial, going to the court of appeals 
via rule 65 of the rules of court, petition 
for certiorari, and based on grave abuse 
of discretion. 
b) new and material issues are raised which 
were not previously presented before the 
Department of Justice and were not hence 
ruled upon;  
c) the prescription of the offense is not due to 
lapse within six months from notice of the 
questioned resolution; and  
d) The appeal or petition for review is filed 
within 30 days from notice 
 
If there is already a resolution of the Office of the 
President, then it can be raised to the Supreme Court 
within 15 days from notice of the final order of the 
Office of the President, and the procedure that you will 
take will be under Rule 43 of the Rules of Court. The 
resolution in the Court of Appeals, can be raised to the 
Supreme Court via rule 45 to the Supreme Court. 
 
From the Office of the President (can be raised by) 
Rule 43 because the Office of the President is 
considered quasi-judicial body when it resolves appeal 
similar to this. From the Court of Appeals, under Rule 
43 whatever decision judgment resolution order there 
that is final can be raised by Rule 45 (Petition for 
Review on Certiorari) which should be based on pure 
questions of law to the Supreme Court. 
 
Review of the ruling of the ombudsman in 
criminal cases 
In criminal cases, the ruling of the Ombudsman shall 
be elevated to the Supreme Court by way of Rule 65 
(Petition for Certiorari). The Supreme Court's power 
of a review over resolutions and orders of the Office 
of the Ombudsman is restricted only to determining 
whether there is a grave abuse of discretion committed 
by the Ombudsman in the resolution of criminal cases. 
The court is not authorized to correct every error or 
mistake of the Office of the Ombudsman other than 
grave abuse of discretion. The remedy therefore is not 
a petition under Rule 45 but a Petition for Certiorari 
under Rule 65. 
 
Appeal from the Resolution of the Prosecutor in 
Election Offense 
In cases where the prosecutor or city fiscal exercises 
the delegated power to conduct a preliminary 
investigation of election offenses, the issue of probable 
cause is resolved by the investigating officer after 
submitting their recommendation. From such 
resolution, appeal to the COMELEC lies. Such second 
look at the probable cause is an exercise of the 
COMELEC’s review powers. The COMELEC’S 
ruling then would be considered immediately final and 
executory. As the COMELEC is acting as a quasi-
judicial body, any appeals to such ruling is raised to the 
Court of Appeals via Rule 43. 
 
There is a concept of the court’s retention of 
jurisdiction upon the filing of the information. 
Whatever the remedy is after the determination of 
probable cause or the lack thereof, if it is the 
determination of probable cause, there is no stopping 
the public prosecutor from filing the information even 
if the aggrieved party raises it to the Secretary of Justice 
(SOJ). 
 
The determination of the presence or absence of 
probable cause for the issuance of warrants of arrest 
against the accused or for the withdrawal of the 
information or for the dismissal of the case are also 
under the court’s discretion. 
 
Where it has already been filed with the court and there 
has been a review in the SOJ, before there is a 
resolution by the SOJ, a motion for suspension must 
be filed in the trial court. This is done to avoid 
simultaneous proceedings because resolutions of the 
trial court are as valid as that of the SOJ. Thus, being 
separate entities, simultaneous proceedings might lead 
to conflicting resolutions. When the information is 
filed with the trial court, it shall be its duty and 
discretion that will prevail. 
 
Section 4. Resolution of investigating prosecutor 
and its review. — If the investigating prosecutor 
finds cause to hold the respondent for trial, he shall 
prepare the resolution and information. He shall 
certify under oath in the information that he, or as 
shown by the record, an authorized officer, has 
personally examined the complainant and his 
witnesses; that there is reasonable ground to believe 
that a crime has been committed and that the 
accused is probably guilty thereof; that the accused 
was informed of the complaint and of the evidence 
submitted against him; and that he was given an 
opportunity to submit controverting evidence. 
Otherwise, he shall recommend the dismissal of the 
complaint. 
 
Within five (5) days from his resolution, he shall 
forward the record of the case to the provincial or 
city prosecutor or chief state prosecutor, or to the 
Ombudsman or his deputy in cases of offenses 
cognizable by the Sandiganbayan in the exercise of 
its original jurisdiction. They shall act on the 
resolution within ten (10) days from their receipt 
thereof and shall immediately inform the parties of 
such action. 
 
No complaint or information may be filed or 
dismissed by an investigating prosecutor without the 
prior written authority or approval of the provincial 
or city prosecutor or chief state prosecutor or the 
Ombudsman or his deputy.

48 
 
Where the investigating prosecutor recommends the 
dismissal of the complaint but his recommendation 
is disapproved by the provincial or city prosecutor 
or chief state prosecutor or the Ombudsman or his 
deputy on the ground that a probable cause exists, 
the latter may, by himself, file the information 
against the respondent, or direct any other assistant 
prosecutor or state prosecutor to do so without 
conducting another preliminary investigation. 
 
If upon petition by a proper party under such rules 
as the Department of Justice may prescribe or motu 
proprio, the Secretary of Justice reverses or modifies 
the resolution of the provincial or city prosecutor or 
chief state prosecutor, he shall direct the prosecutor 
concerned either to file the corresponding 
information without conducting another preliminary 
investigation, or to dismiss or move for dismissal of 
the complaint or information with notice to the 
parties. The same rule shall apply in preliminary 
investigations conducted by the officers of the 
Office of the Ombudsman. (4a) 
 
Section 5. When warrant of arrest may issue.* —  
(a) By the Regional Trial Court. — Within ten (10) 
days from the filing of the complaint or information, 
the judge shall personally evaluate the resolution of 
the prosecutor and its supporting evidence. He may 
immediately dismiss the case if the evidence on 
record clearly fails to establish probable cause. If he 
finds probable cause, he shall issue a warrant of 
arrest, or a commitment order when the complaint 
or information was filed pursuant to section 6 of this 
Rule. In case of doubt on the existence of probable 
cause, the judge may order the prosecutor to present 
additional evidence within five (5) days from notice 
and the issue must be resolved by the court within 
thirty (30) days from the filing of the complaint or 
information.  
(b) By the Municipal Trial Court. — When required 
pursuant to the second paragraph of section 1 of this 
Rule, the preliminary investigation of cases falling 
under the original jurisdiction of the Metropolitan 
Trial Court, Municipal Trial Court in Cities, 
Municipal Trial Court or Municipal Circuit Trial 
Court SHALL be conducted by the prosecutor. The 
procedure for the issuance of a warrant of arrest by 
the judge shall be governed by paragraph (a) of this 
section.  
(c) When warrant of arrest not necessary. — A 
warrant of arrest shall not issue if the accused is 
already under detention pursuant to a warrant issued 
by the municipal trial court in accordance with 
paragraph (b) of this section, or if the complaint or 
information was filed pursuant to section 6 of this 
Rule or is for an offense penalized by fine only. The 
court shall then proceed in the exercise of its original 
jurisdiction. (As amended by A.M. No. 05-8-26- SC.) 
 
Warrant of arrest 
Is a written order of the court, which is made on behalf 
of the State and is based upon a complaint issued 
pursuant to statute and/or court rule; and which 
commands law enforcement officers to arrest a person 
and bring him before a magistrate. 
 
Action of the judge upon filing of the complaint or 
information 
In the procedure for the issuance of a warrant, the 
judge of the RTC shall within 10 days from the filing 
of the complaint or information do either of the 
following:  
1) The judge of the RTC shall personally evaluate 
the resolution of the prosecutor and the 
supporting evidence regarding the existence of 
probable cause, and on the basis thereof, issue 
a warrant of arrest. 
 
Warrant of arrest Search warrant 
the judge will personally 
look at the documents, 
information, and 
supporting evidence, etc. 
the judge will require a 
personal examination of 
the complainant or the 
one that applied for a 
search warrant, and the 
witnesses which he may 
provide. 
 
2) The trial court judge of the RTC, if he has 
doubts as to the existence of probable cause, 
shall have the power to order the prosecutor to 
present additional evidence. 
 
 Additional evidence - The judge must 
satisfy himself that based on the 
evidence submitted there is sufficient 
proof that a crime has been committed 
and that the person to be arrested is 
probably guilty thereof. At this stage of 
the criminal proceeding, the judge is 
not yet tasked to review in detail the 
evidence submitted during the 
preliminary investigation. It is 
sufficient that he personally evaluates 
such evidence in determining probable 
cause. 
 Dismiss - The judge is also empowered 
to immediately dismiss the case if the 
evidence on record clearly fails to 
establish probable cause. But it is only 
when the judge finds that the evidence 
on hand is absolutely failing to support 
a finding of probable cause, that he can 
dismiss the case. 
 Warrant of arrest with 
recommendation for bail - When there 
is a determination by the judge that 
there is probable cause, a judge should 
issue a warrant of arrest with a 
recommendation for bail — in those 
cases of course that bail is a matter of 
right. 
 
Rule 114, Section 17. Bail, where filed. (c) Any 
person in custody who is not yet charged in court

SUMMARY 
(1) He may immediately dismiss the case, if the 
evidence on the record clearly fails to 
establish probable cause; or  
(2) If he finds probable cause, he shall issue a 
warrant of arrest or a commitment order if 
the complaint or information was filed 
pursuant to Section 6 of Rule 112 (valid 
warrantless arrest); or  
(3) If he doubts the existence of probable cause, 
he may order the prosecutor to present 
additional evidence within five days from 
notice 
 
In relation to the discussion that it is the duty of the 
judge of the court to personally evaluate the records of 
the case, the judge should not rely solely on the 
certification of the fiscal. 
 
When is Warrant of Arrest unnecessary? 
1) If the complaint is filed pursuant to the 
provision of section 6 rule 112 due to the 
absence or unavailability of an inquest 
prosecutor. the issuance of a warrant is 
unnecessary because the accused is already in 
detention. What the court will issue is a 
commitment order. 
2) If the complaint or information was filed after 
inquest proceeding conducted by the inquest 
prosecutor. Same as #1, here there is already 
detention of the accused, and therefore the 
court will just issue a commitment order 
instead of a warrant of arrest 
3) In criminal cases falling under the Revised 
Rules on Summary Procedure except when the 
accused failed to appear whenever required. 
This is because for cases falling under the 
Revised Rules on Summary Procedure, there is 
usually no imprisonment. The penalty is mostly 
fine. 
XPN: However, if the person accused of a 
crime failed to appear whenever required by 
the court, then the court may issue a warrant of 
arrest to compel him to come to court 
4) In cases not requiring preliminary investigation 
nor falling under the Revised Rule on Summary 
Procedure and the judge is satisfied that there 
is no necessity for placing the accused under 
custody 
5) When the evidence on record fails clearly to 
establish probable cause; and 
6) When the offense is penalized by fine only 
 
Section 6, Rule 112. When accused lawfully arrested 
without warrant. — When a person is lawfully 
arrested without a warrant involving an offense 
which requires a preliminary investigation, the 
complaint or information may be filed by a 
prosecutor without need of such investigation 
provided an inquest has been conducted in 
accordance with existing rules. 
 
In the absence or unavailability of an inquest 
prosecutor, the complaint may be filed by the 
offended party or a peace office directly with the 
proper court on the basis of the affidavit of the 
offended party or arresting officer or person.  
 
Before the complaint or information is filed, the 
person arrested may ask for a preliminary 
investigation in accordance with this Rule, but he 
must sign a waiver of the provisions of Article 125 
of the Revised Penal Code, as amended, in the 
presence of his counsel. Notwithstanding the 
waiver, he may apply for bail and the investigation 
must be terminated within fifteen (15) days from its 
inception.  
 
After the filing of the complaint or information in 
court without a preliminary investigation, the 
accused may, within five (5) days from the time he 
learns of its filing, ask for a preliminary investigation 
with the same right to adduce evidence in his defense 
as provided in this Rule. 
 
INQUEST PROCEEDINGS 
Application 
Applies only in cases where the accused was lawfully 
arrested without warrant of arrest under Section 5 Rule 
113 of the Revised Rules of Criminal Procedure. 
Where the records do not show that the accused was 
lawfully arrested, this rule does not apply. 
 
Under DOJ Circular No. 61, dated September 21 1993, 
the initial duty of the inquest officer is to determine if 
the arrest of the detained person was made in 
accordance with the provisions of paragraphs A and B 
of Section 5 of rule 113. 
 
DOJ Circular No. 61, Section 9. Where arrest not 
properly effected. — Should the Inquest Officer find 
that the arrest was not made in accordance with the 
Rules, he shall:  
a. recommend the release of the person arrested 
or detained;  
b. note down the disposition of the referral 
document;  
c. prepare a brief memorandum indicating the 
reasons for the action taken; and  
d. forward the same, together with the record of 
the case, to the City or Provincial Prosecutor for 
appropriate action.  
 
Where the recommendation for the release of the 
detained person is approved by the City or 
Provincial Prosecutor but the evidence on hand 
warrant the conduct of a regular preliminary 
investigation, the order of release shall be served on 
the officer having custody of said detainee and shall 
direct the said officer to serve upon the detainee the 
subpoena or notice of preliminary investigation, 
together with the copies of the charge sheet or 
complaint, affidavits or sworn statements of the 

--- PAGE 50 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
50 
complainant and his witnesses and other supporting 
evidence. 
 
2017 Revised Manual for Prosecutors, Section 3.1.1. 
Concept — An inquest proceeding is an informal 
and summary investigation conducted by a public 
prosecutor in criminal cases involving persons 
arrested and detained without the benefit of a 
warrant of arrest issued by the court for the purpose 
of determining whether or not these persons 
should remain under custody and 
correspondingly be charged in court 
 
2017 Revised Manual for Prosecutors, Section 3.1.2. 
Coverage — The conduct of inquest proceedings 
covers the following:  
a. All offenses covered under the Revised Penal 
Code and special laws, rules and regulations which 
requires a preliminary investigation;  
b. Where respondent is a minor (below eighteen 
[18] years old), the inquest proceedings shall cover 
only offenses punishable by imprisonment of not 
less than six (6) years and one (1) day, provided 
that no inquest investigation shall be conducted 
unless the child–respondent shall have first 
undergone the requisite proceedings before the 
Local Social Welfare Development Officer 
pursuant to the Rules on Inquest with Respect to 
Children in Conflict With the Law (CICL). 
 
2017 Revised Manual for Prosecutors, Section 3.1.3. 
Designation of an Inquest Prosecutor. – The 
Prosecutor General or the Provincial/City 
Prosecutor shall designate the prosecutors assigned 
to inquest duties and their schedule of assignments. 
 
2017 Revised Manual for Prosecutors, Section 3.1.4. 
Venue of Inquest Cases. – Unless otherwise directed 
by the Prosecutor General or the Provincial/City 
Prosecutor, those assigned to inquest duties shall 
discharge their functions during the hours of their 
designated assignments at the office of the inquest 
prosecutor in order to expedite and facilitate the 
disposition of inquest cases, unless otherwise 
directed by the Head of Office. 
 
2017 Revised Manual for Prosecutors, Section 3.2. 
Procedure. – All complaints for inquest shall be 
received and docketed by the prosecution office 
concerned.  
 
Section 3.2.1. Commencement. – The inquest 
proceedings shall be considered commenced 
upon receipt by the inquest prosecutor of the 
following documents:  
a. Affidavit of arrest duly subscribed and sworn to 
before him/her by the arresting officer;  
b. Investigation report;  
c. Sworn statements of the complainant/s and 
witness/es; and  
d. Other supporting pieces of evidence gathered 
by the law enforcement authorities in the course of 
their investigation.  
 
Section 3.2.2. Incomplete Documents. – When the 
documents presented are incomplete to establish 
probable cause, the inquest prosecutor shall direct 
the law enforcement authorities to submit, in 
the proper form, the required evidence within 
the period prescribed under the provisions of 
Article 125 of the Revised Penal Code, as 
amended. Failure to submit the required evidence 
within the prescribed period shall constrain the 
inquest prosecutor to order the release of the 
detained person/s. 
 
Section 3.2.3. Instances Where the Presence of the 
Detained Person is Dispensed With. – The presence of 
the detained person shall be ensured during the 
proceedings except in the following cases:  
a. If he is confined in a hospital; or  
b. If he is detained in a place under maximum 
security.  
 
The inquest prosecutor shall reflect the absence of 
the detained person for any of the foregoing reasons 
in the record of the case. 
 
Section 3.2.4. Charges and Counter-Charges. – All 
charges and counter-charges arising from the same 
incident shall, as far as practicable, be jointly 
investigated and the conduct of the inquest 
proceedings be terminated, and be referred for 
further preliminary investigation. 
 
Section 3.2.6. Where the Arrest of the Detained Person 
was Properly Effected. – Should the inquest prosecutor 
find that the arrest was properly effected, the 
detained person shall be asked if he/she desires to 
avail himself/herself of a preliminary investigation 
and, if he/she does, the consequences thereof must 
be explained to him/her adequately.  
 
 
The detained person must be assisted by a lawyer of 
his/her own choice. If he/she has none, the inquest 
prosecutor shall provide him/her the services of a 
public attorney.  
 
The detained person, assisted by his/her lawyer, 
shall then be made to execute a waiver of the 
provisions of Article 125 of the Revised Penal Code, 
as amended.  
 
The preliminary investigation may be conducted by 
the inquest prosecutor himself/herself or by any 
other prosecutor to whom the case may be assigned 
by the Prosecutor General or the Provincial/City 
Prosecutor, which investigation shall be terminated 
within fifteen (15) days from its commencement. 
 
2017 Revised Manual for Prosecutors, Section 3.3 
Inquest Proper. – Where the detained person does 
not opt for a preliminary investigation or otherwise 
refuses to execute the required waiver, the inquest 
prosecutor shall proceed with the conduct of the 
inquest proceeding by examining the sworn 
statements/ affidavits of the complainant and the 
witnesses and other supporting evidence submitted. 
The conduct of an inquest proceeding should 

--- PAGE 51 ---
  FROM THE ANNOTATIONS OF DEAN TAN AND DISCUSSIONS OF ATTY DERIJE 
 
            Criminal Procedure | VENTEROSO 
51 
never be initiated in the absence of an affidavit 
of arrest.  
 
If necessary, the inquest prosecutor shall require the 
presence of the complaining witnesses and subject 
this witness to an informal and summary 
investigation or examination for the purposes of 
determining the existence of probable cause.  
 
Section 3.3.1. Action to be Taken When there is a finding 
of Probable Cause. – Probable cause has been defined 
as the existence of such facts and circumstances as 
would excite the belief, in a reasonable mind, acting 
on the facts within the knowledge of the prosecutor, 
that the person charged was guilty of the crime for 
which he/she was prosecuted. Probable cause is a 
reasonable ground of presumption that a matter is, 
or may be, well-founded, such a state of facts in the 
mind of the prosecutor as would lead a person of 
ordinary caution and prudence to believe, or 
entertain an honest or strong suspicion, that a thing 
is so. 
 
If the inquest prosecutor finds that probable cause 
exists, he/she shall forthwith prepare the resolution 
with the corresponding Complaint/Information 
with the recommendation that the same be filed in 
court. The Complaint/Information shall indicate the 
offense/s committed and the amount of bail 
recommended, if applicable. However, in inquest 
cases for crimes covered by the Rules on Summary 
Procedure and Republic Act No. 603610 where no 
bail is required, the inquest prosecutor shall 
recommend the release of the arrested person and 
prepare the Information for filing with the court.  
 
Thereafter, the record of the case, together with the 
resolution and the Complaint/Information, shall be 
forwarded to the Prosecutor General or the 
Provincial/City Prosecutor for approval and 
subsequent filing before the proper Court. 
 
Section 3.3.2. Action to be Taken When There is an 
Absence of Probable Cause. – If the inquest prosecutor 
finds no probable cause to indict the 
arrested/detained person, he/she shall:  
 
a. Recommend the release of the arrested or 
detained person;  
b. Prepare a resolution of dismissal indicating 
therein the reason/s for the action taken; and  
c. Forward the record of the case to the 
Prosecutor General or the Provincial/City 
Prosecutor for their appropriate action. 
 
GR: the inquest proceedings must be terminated 
within the period prescribed, which will be counted 
from the time the arrest under the provisions of Article 
125 of the Revised Penal Code 
XPN: When there is a waiver for the purpose of 
preliminary investigation 
 
Revised Penal Code 
Art. 125. Delay in the delivery of detained persons to the 
proper judicial authorities. — The penalties provided in 
the next preceding article shall be imposed upon the 
public officer or employee who shall detain any 
person for some legal ground and shall fail to deliver 
such person to the proper judicial authorities within 
the period of; twelve (12) hours, for crimes or 
offenses punishable by light penalties, or their 
equivalent; eighteen (18) hours, for crimes or 
offenses punishable by correctional penalties, or 
their equivalent and thirty-six (36) hours, for 
crimes, or offenses punishable by afflictive or 
capital penalties, or their equivalent. 
 
In every case, the person detained shall be informed 
of the cause of his detention and shall be allowed 
upon his request, to communicate and confer at any 
time with his attorney or counsel. 
 
In case of  
1) violation of the Human Security Act within 72 
hours, which is now extended to 14 calendar 
days under the Anti-Terror Law. 
2) violation of the anti-smuggling laws, and for 
violations of the dangerous drugs act, if the 
result of the inquest leads to dismissal, then it 
is subject to automatic review to the 
Secretary of Justice. 
3) For Sandiganbayan cases, should any 
complaint cognizable by the Sandiganbayan be 
referred to the Office of the Prosecutor 
General or City/Provincial Prosecutor's Office 
for the conduct of inquest proceedings, the 
latter shall refrain from accepting the same 
and shall advise the law enforcer to file the 
complaint before the Office of the 
Ombudsman or the Office of the Special 
Prosecutor through any of the branch clerk of 
court in the locality concerned

the proper court on the basis of the affidavit of the

Section 7. Records. —  
(a) Records supporting the information or 
complaint. — An information or complaint filed in 
court shall be supported by the affidavits and 
counter-affidavits of the parties and their witnesses, 
together with the other supporting evidence and the 
resolution on the case.  
 
(b) Record of preliminary investigation. — The 
record of the preliminary investigation conducted by 
a prosecutor OR OTHER OFFICERS AS MAY BE 
AUTHORIZED BY LAW shall not form part of 
the record of the case. However, the court, on its 
own initiative or on motion of any party, may order 
the production of the record or any of its part when 
necessary in the resolution of the case or any 
incident therein, or when it is to be introduced as an 
evidence in the case by the requesting party. (8a) (As 
amended by A.M. No. 05-8-26-SC). 
 
1) Records supporting the information or 
complaint - will be taken judicial notice of by 
the court and there needs no other proof as to 
its existence 
2) Records of the preliminary investigation - 
not included in the document supporting the 
complaint or information, must be duly 
presented in court 
 
Section 8. Cases not requiring a preliminary investigation 
nor covered by the Rule on Summary Procedure. —  
(a) If filed with the prosecutor. — If the complaint 
is filed directly with the prosecutor involving an 
offense punishable by an imprisonment of less than 
four (4) years, two (2) months and one (1) day, 
the procedure outlined in section 3(a) of this Rule 
shall be observed. The prosecutor shall act on the 
complaint based on the affidavits and other 
supporting documents submitted by the 
complainant within ten (10) days from its filing.  
 
(b) If filed with the Municipal Trial Court. — If 
the complaint or information is filed with the 
Municipal Trial Court or Municipal Circuit Trial 
Court for an offense covered by this section, the 
procedure in section 3(a) of this rule shall be 
observed. If within ten (10) days after the filing of 
the complaint of information, the judge finds no 
probable cause after personally evaluating the 
evidence, or after personally examining in 
writing and under oath the complainant and his 
witnesses in the form of searching questions 
and answers, he shall dismiss the same. He may, 
however, require the submission of additional 
evidence, within ten (10) days from notice, to 
determine further the existence of probable 
cause. If the judge still finds no probable cause 
despite the additional evidence, he shall, within ten 
(10) days from its submission or expiration of said 
period, dismiss the case. When he finds probable 
cause, he shall issue a warrant of arrest, or a 
commitment order if the accused had already 
been arrested, and hold him for trial. However, if 
the judge is satisfied that there is no necessity for 
placing the accused under custody, he may issue 
summons instead of a warrant of arrest. (9a) 
 
Rule 112, Section 3. Procedure. — The preliminary 
investigation shall be conducted in the following 
manner:  
(a) If filed with the prosecutor. — The complaint 
shall state the address of the respondent and shall 
be accompanied by the affidavits of the 
complainant and his witnesses, as well as other 
supporting documents to establish probable 
cause. They shall be in such number of copies as 
there are respondents, plus two (2) copies for the 
official file. The affidavits shall be subscribed and 
sworn to before any prosecutor or government 
official authorized to administer oath, or, in their 
absence or unavailability, before a notary public, 
each of who must certify that he personally 
examined the affiants and that he is satisfied that 
they voluntarily executed and understood their 
affidavits. 
 
Application 
Includes cases which do not require the conduct of a 
preliminary investigation. These are offenses  
1) with the penalty prescribed by law as 
imprisonment for not more than 4 years and 2 
months, without regard to fine; or  
2) those offenses covered by a summary 
procedure which we will know by process of 
elimination. 
 
Rules 
Under Section 3(A), the prosecutor doesn’t need to 
follow b,c,d,e,and f. The determination of the probable 
cause under cases which do not require preliminary 
investigation; may rely solely on the complaints and the 
affidavits and supporting evidences provided for by the 
complainant. But this is not to say that public 
prosecutor is constrained to just rely on the

within 10 days, without asking for a counteraffidavit 
from the respondent 
 
Under Sec 8(B) (If filed with the Municipal Trial

Metropolitan Trial Court, or a First Level Court. 
Because it is already filed in a court of law, it is already 
judicial in nature; the proceedings that will be 
undertaken by the judge will be considered a 
preliminary investigation.
